SECTION 101. GENERAL 

Page 823 

The Veterans' Administration was created by Executive Order 5398, dated July 21, 1930, under authorization of the act of Congress approved July 3, 1930 (46 Stat. 1016; 38 U. S. C. 11). This act authorized the President to consolidate and coordinate under a single control all Federal agencies dealing with veterans' affairs. The order consolidated in the Veterans' Administration, the Bureau of Pensions (formerly under the Secretary of the Interior), the United States 

Veterans Bureau, and the National Home for Disabled Volunteer

Soldiers. The Veterans' Administration is an independent establishment under the President.

The Veterans' Administration administers laws relating to the relief of, and other benefits provided by law for, former members of the military and naval forces. It is responsible for extending relief to veterans and to dependents of deceased veterans of all wars, and to veterans and to dependents of deceased veterans who served in the Military and Naval Establishments during time of peace, as provided for by various acts of Congress. The benefits administered by the Veterans' Administration include compensation, pensions, vocational rehabilitation and education, the guaranty of loans for purchase or construction of homes, farms, and business property, readjustment allowance for veterans who are unemployed, Government life insurance, death benefits, adjusted compensation, emergency officers' retirement pay, and physical examinations, hospital and outpatient treatment, and domiciliary care. The Veterans' Administration also administers the insurance article of the Soldiers' and Sailors' Civil Relief Act of 1940, as amended (50 U. S. C. App. 540-548, inclusive), for persons in the active military service.

The Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944, as amended, declares the Veterans' Administration to be an essential war agency and entitled to priority equal to the highest granted any department or agency of the Government in personnel, service, space, equipment, supplies, and material under any laws, Executive orders, and regu lations pertaining to priorities. The Administrator of Veterans' 

Affairs is authorized, for the purpose of extending benefits to veterans and dependents, and to the extent he deems necessary, to procure the necessary space for administrative, clinical, medical, and out-patienttreatment purposes by lease, purchase, or construction of buildings, or by condemnation or declaration of taking, pursuant to existing statutes (38 U. S. C. 693). 1

SECTION 102. ORGANIZATION 

The Veterans' Administration is composed of three different classes of organizational units: 

(a) The Central office. 

(b) The Branch offices. 

(c) The Field stations. 

SECTION 103. CENTRAL OFFICE 

The Office of the Administrator consists of the Administrator and his personal office organization. In the Office of the Executive 

Assistant Administrator are the following: 

Assistant Executive Assistant Administrators.

Budget Service. 

Coordination Service. 

Foreign Relations Service. 

Inspection and Investigation Service. 

Information Service. 

The staff units of the organization are as follows: 

Office of the Assistant Administrator for Claims.

Office of the Assistant Administrator for Construction, Supply, and Real Estate.

Office of the Assistant Administrator for Contact and Administrative Services. 

Office of the Assistant Administrator for Finance. 

Office of the Assistant Administrator for Insurance. 

Office of the Assistant Administrator for Legislation.

Office of the Chief Medical Director, Department of Medicine and Surgery. 

Office of the Assistant Administrator for Personnel.

Office of the Solicitor. 

Office of the Assistant Administrator for Special Services. 

Office of the Chairman, Board of Veterans' Appeals. 

Office of the Assistant Administrator for Vocational Rehabili tation and Education.

SECTION 104. BRANCH OFFICES 

Thirteen branch offices have been established throughout the United States, each branch office functioning under the supervision of a deputy administrator. Through the deputy administrators the Administrator has delegated authority to discharge effectively the major operating functions of the Veterans' Administration in all functional phases, including hospitalization, adjudication of claims, insurance. finance, personnel, construction and supply, vocational training, education, or rehabilitation, and recreation and morale. 

The cities in which the branch offices are located and the areas assigned to each branch office are as follows:

SECTION 105. FIELD STATIONS 

Louisiana. Mississippi. Texas. Idaho. Montana. Oregon. Washington. Alaska. Arizona. California. Nevada. Hawaii. Colorado. 

New Mexico. Utah. Wyoming.

The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs is authorized and directed by the Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944, as amended, to establish necessary regional offices, suboffices, branch offices, contact. units, or other subordinate offices in centers of population where there is no Veterans' Administration facility, or where such a facility is not readily available or accessible (38 U. S. C. 693a).

Veterans' Administration activities are located in each State and in Alaska, Canal Zone, Guam, Hawaii, Puerto Rico, and the Philippine Islands to expedite the furnishing of benefits provided for veterans and their dependents.

The term "field stations" applies to Veterans' Administration installations located in the field and includes all Veterans' Administration hospitals, centers, domiciliary centers, regional offices, Veterans' Administration offices, and specified special offices which are located in the United States and its Territories or possessions, under jurisdiction of the branch offices. The exceptions to the above are such of these field stations located outside the United States-in the Philippines and in the Canal Zone-which are under jurisdiction of central office. Supply depots, forms depots, and records center are under jurisdiction of central office.

A regional office is an organizational element which under properly constituted authority grants benefits and services provided by law for veterans, their dependents, and beneficiaries within an assigned territory; furnishes information as to all Veterans' Administration benefits and services; procures data regarding applications and claims; rates and adjudicates claims and makes awards for disability compensation and pension; conducts physical and mental examinations for claims purposes; establishes eligibility and need for hospitalization in other Government and private institutions and State-home care; renders out-patient treatment and social service; handles guardianship and fiduciary matters and authorized legal proceedings; aids, guides, and prescribes vocational rehabilitation training and administers educational benefits for World War II veterans; guarantees loans for

World War II veterans for purchase or construction of homes, farms, or business property; aids and otherwise assists veterans in exercising their rights to benefits and services; conducts administrative, finance, supply, files, and records activities; and supervises Veterans' Administration offices under its jurisdiction. 

A Veterans' Administration center is a field station having hospital activities and in addition either domiciliary activities or regional office activities, or both. A domiciliary center is a field station having only domiciliary (home) activities.

The Administrator is also authorized to place officials and employees designated by him in such Army and Navy installations as may be deemed advisable for the purpose of adjudicating disability claims of, and giving aid and advice to, members of the Army and Navy who are about to be discharged or released from active service (38 U. S. C. 693c).

The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs is authorized and directed to expedite and complete the construction of additional hospitals for war veterans and to enter into agreements and contracts for the use by, or transfer to, the Veterans' Administration of suitable Army and Navy hospitals after such institutions are no longer needed by the armed services (38 U. S. C. 693a).

The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs and the Secretary of the 

Army and Secretary of the Navy are granted authority to enter into agreements and contracts for the mutual use or exchange of use of hospital and domiciliary facilities and such supplies, equipment, and material as may be needed to properly operate such facilities or for the transfer, without reimbursement of appropriations, of facilities, supplies, equipment, or material necessary and proper for authorized care for veterans, except that at no time shall the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs enter into any agreement which will result in a permanent reduction of Veterans Administration hospital and domiciliary beds below the number established or approved on June 22, 1944, plus the estimated number required to meet the load of eligibles under laws administered by the Veterans' Administration, or in any way subordinate or transfer the operation of the Veterans' Administration to any other agency of the Government (38 U. S. C. 693b).

United States Army engineers are cooperating with the Veterans' Administration in the largest hospital construction program in history. This program calls for construction of 90 of the latest type fireproof hospitals strategically located for modern medical service, the estimated cost of the program being approximately $1,000,000,000. 

There are now 126 Veterans' Administration hospitals in operation with an authorized bed capacity of 110,000. After completion of the hospital construction program, the Veterans' Administration will have a total of 191 permanent hospitals of all types, with a total bed capacity of approximately 140,000.

As a temporary measure to relieve the present and prospective bed shortage during the construction period, arrangements were made with the armed forces for acquisition of certain surplus hospitals and supplies. The most difficult problem in this connection is obtaining staffs of doctors, nurses, attendants, and other personnel with which to operate these surplus hospitals. The Veterans' Administration has acquired 35 surplus hospitals from the armed services, 28 of which

10 (approximately 21,000 beds) are for temporary use, and is now in - process of activating 3 additional Army installations which will add 1,500 domiciliary beds. Approximately 12,500 patients are hospitalized in other than Veterans' Administration hospitals on a contract basis.

During 1948 the out-patient medical program has been effectively continued, supported by the "home town" medical care plan. At the present time, the plan is in effect in Hawaii, Alaska, and 38 States (all States except Rhode Island, Nebraska, Mississippi, Texas, New 

Mexico, Maryland, Vermont, Louisiana, Utah, and Alabama). During the first 6 months of 1948 about 14 percent of all out-patient medical examinations and 51 percent of all out-patient medical treatments were being done by fee-designated physicians and the remainder by Veterans' Administration staff doctors.

Veterans' Administration offices have been established throughout the United States under managers responsible to the parent regional office to render services locally to veterans, their dependents and beneficiaries which cannot be furnished conveniently by the regional office. 

These offices perform such regional office functions as may be assigned to facilitate the granting of benefits to veterans and beneficiaries, furnishing information and assistance to veterans and others, including those in the armed forces, as to rights and benefits provided by law and regulations. The nearest Veterans' Administration office may be located by reference to the local telephone directory, by inquiry at the local post office, or by writing the nearest Veterans' Administration center or regional office.

CHAPTER II SEPARATION FROM THE ARMED FORCES AND STATUS Section OF PARTICULAR GROUPS 

201. Honorable discharge, minority or misrepresentation of age. 

202. Rights under veterans' laws barred by certain types of discharge. 

203. Review of type and nature of service member's discharge or dismissal from military or naval forces__ 

204. Correction of military and naval records.. 

205. Status of particular groups_- Page 10 11 11 

SECTION 201. HONORABLE DISCHARGE, MINORITY, OR MISREPRE- SENTATION OF AGE 

The act of March 3, 1936 (10 U. S. C. 654b), provides that in the administration of any laws conferring rights, privileges, or benefits upon honorably discharged soldiers of the United States Army, their widows, and dependent children, a soldier who served as an enlisted man between April 6, 1917, and November 11, 1918, who was discharged for fraudulent enlistment on account of minority or misrepresentation of age, should be held to have been discharged honorably from the military service on the date of his actual separation therefrom if his service otherwise entitles him to an honorable discharge. The act provides for the furnishing of discharge certificates by the service department upon request of the veterans or their widows, showing that the soldier is held and considered to have been honorably discharged under the provisions of such act. 

The act of February 9, 1940 (34 U. S. C. 205, 697), provides a similar benefit for former sailors or marines of the United States Navy or 

Marine Corps. The Navy Department will issue discharge certificates upon request of veterans, or their widows. 

The act of May 25, 1937 (10 U. S. C. 655), provides a similar benefit for former soldiers of the United States Army, who enlisted between April 21, 1898, and July 4, 1902, and their widows and dependent children, and the act of June 22, 1938 (34 U. S. C. 206, 696), provides a similar benefit for former members of the United States Navy or 

Marine Corps, who enlisted between April 21, 1898, and July 4, 1902, and their widows and dependent children.

SECTION 202. RIGHTS UNDER VETERANS' LAWS BARRED BY CERTAIN TYPES OF DISCHARGE 

The discharge or dismissal by reason of a sentence of a general court martial of any person from the military or naval forces, or the discharge of any such person on the ground that he was a conscientious objector who refused to perform military duty or refused to wear the uniform or otherwise to comply with lawful orders of competent 81200°-48-

military authority, or as a deserter, or of an officer by the acceptance of his resignation for the good of the service, shall bar all rights of such person, based upon the period of service from which he is so discharged or dismissed, under any laws administered by the Veterans' Administration. However, in the case of any such person, if it is established to the satisfaction of the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs that at the time of the commission of the offense such person was insane, he may not be precluded from benefits to which he is otherwise entitled under the laws administered by the Veterans' 

Administration. 

This provision does not apply to any war risk, Government (converted), or national service life-insurance policy (sec. 300, Public 

Law 346, 78th Cong., as amended (38 U. S. C. 693g)).

A discharge or release from active service under conditions other than dishonorable is a prerequisite to entitlement to veterans' benefits provided by the Servicemen's Readjustment Act, as amended, or Public Law 2, Seventy-third Congress, as amended (sec. 1503, Public Law 

346, 78th Cong., June 22, 1944 (38 U. S. C. 697c)).

It will be noted that this section of the law does not extend veterans' benefits to persons not dishonorably discharged but to those whose active service was terminated under conditions other than dishonorable.

A discharge or separation from active duty will be considered to be under "dishonorable conditions" if such discharge or separation from active military or naval service was (1) for mutiny, (2) spying, or (3) for an offense involving moral turpitude or willful and persistent misconduct. Acceptance of an undesirable or blue discharge to escape trial by a general court martial is also a bar to benefits, the discharge being considered to be under dishonorable conditions. Generally, an undesirable or blue discharge issued because of homosexual acts or tendencies is a bar to benefits, but the facts in a particular case may warrant a different conclusion. A veteran discharged for alienage during a period of hostilities is ineligible for benefits, unless he can establish that it was not pursuant to his own request. However, such a veteran, if discharged for alienage after the termination of hostilities and if his service was honest and faithful is not barred from benefits if he is found to be otherwise entitled thereto. As pointed out in section 203, certain discharges may be reviewed by the service departments with a view to having the nature thereof changed so as to make the veteran eligible for benefits from the Veterans' Administration. 

Where service was otherwise honest, faithful, and meritorious a discharge or separation other than dishonorable because of the commission of a minor offense will not be deemed to constitute discharge or separation under dishonorable conditions. 

SECTION 203. REVIEW OF TYPE AND NATURE OF SERVICE MEMBER'S DISCHARGE OR DISMISSAL FROM MILITARY OR NAVAL FORCES 

The Secretary of the Army, the Secretary of the Air Force, and the Secretary of the Navy, after conference with the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs, are authorized and directed to establish in the departments, respectively, boards of review composed of five members each, whose duties are to review, on their own motion or upon the request of a former officer or enlisted man or woman or, if deceased, by

the surviving spouse, next of kin, or legal representative, the type and nature of his discharge or dismissal, except a discharge or dismissal by reason of the sentence of a general court martial. Such review shall be based upon all available records of the service department relating to the person requesting such review, and such other evidence as may be presented by such person. Witnesses shall be permitted to present testimony either in person or by affidavit and the person requesting review shall be allowed to appear before such board in person or by counsel. The term "counsel" as used herein shall be construed to include, among others, accredited representatives of veterans' organizations recognized by the Veterans' Administration under section 

200 of the Act of June 29, 1936 (Public No. 844, 74th Cong.). Such board shall have authority, except in the case of a discharge or dismissal by reason of the sentence of a general court martial, to change, correct, or modify any discharge or dismissal, and to issue a new discharge in accord with the facts presented to the board. The findings of such board are final, subject only to review by the Secretary of the service department concerned. No request for review by such board of a discharge or dismissal under the foregoing provisions shall be valid unless filed within 15 years after such discharge or dismissal or within 15 years after the effective date of the Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944, whichever be the later (38 U. S. C. 693h). For information concerning such review, inquiries of persons who served in the Army should be addressed to the Adjutant General's Office, Department of the Army, Washington, D. C., of those who served in the Navy, to the Bureau of Naval Personnel, Navy Department,

Washington, D. C., and of those who served in the Air Force, to the 

Air Adjutant General, Washington, D. C. 

At such times as the Coast Guard is operating under the Treasury Department similar authority is vested in the Secretary of the Treasury with respect to the discharge or dismissal of former personnel of the Coast Guard. Findings of review boards established for this purpose are final, subject only to review by the Secretary of the Treasury (38 U. S. C. 693h). For information concerning such review, inquiries of persons who served in the Coast Guard should be addressed to The Commandant, Coast Guard, Washington, D. C. 

SECTION 204. CORRECTION OF MILITARY AND NAVAL RECORDS 

The Secretary of the Army, the Secretary of the Navy, and the Secretary of the Treasury with respect to the Coast Guard, respectively, under procedures set up by them, and acting through boards of civilian officers or employees of their respective departments, are authorized to correct any military or naval record where in their judgment such action is necessary to correct an error or to remove an injustice (5 U. S. C. 191a).

Part A. National Service Life Insurance SECTION 301. GENERAL 

19 National service life insurance, administered by the Veterans' Administration, has been available since October 8, 1940, to persons in the active service of the armed forces. With certain exceptions, this insurance is also presently available to persons who were in such active service between October 8, 1940, and September 2, 1945, both dates inclusive, upon application, evidence of good health, and payment of the required premiums.

In any case in which application for life or disability insurance or for reinstatement of such insurance is made prior to January 1, 1950, and the applicant has had active service between October 8, 1940, and 

September 2, 1945, the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs may not deny that the applicant is in good health because of any disability or disabilities, less than total in degree, resulting from or aggravated by such active service.

National service life insurance was issued originally on a 5-year level-premium term plan only, with the privilege of conversion, after the insurance had been in effect for 1 year to insurance on a permanent plan. Under present law such insurance may be issued initially on any of the following plans: 5-year level-premium term, ordinary life, 20-payment life, 30-payment life, 20-year endowment, endowment at age 60, and endowment at age 65.

All national service life insurance issued on a 5-year level-premium term plan on or before December 31, 1945, and not exchanged or converted prior to that time to a permanent plan was extended for an additional period of 3 years at the same premium rate charged for the initial 5-year period. Any national service life insurance issued on a 

5-year level-premium term plan before January 1, 1948, which has not been exchanged or converted to a permanent plan of insurance, may be renewed as level-premium term insurance for an additional 16

period of 5 years at the premium rate for the then attained age without medical examination.

SECTION 302. SPECIFIC PROVISIONS 

National service life insurance policies provide for the waiver of premiums during the continuous total disability of the insured, which continues or has continued for six or more consecutive months, if such disability commenced (1) subsequent to the date of his application for insurance, (2) while the insurance is in force under premium paying conditions, and (3) prior to the insured's sixtieth birthday. 

An additional provision granting benefits on account of total disability may be obtained by the insured upon application and evidence showing the required condition of health and payment of an additional premium. Monthly income payments and waiver of premium are granted under this provision if the insured is totally disabled for a period of six consecutive months or more commencing after the date of such application and before attaining the age of 60 and while payment of any premium is not in default. Such payments do not decrease the face value of the policy.

The insured since August 1, 1946, has had a right to designate any person or persons, firm, corporation, or legal entity (including the estate of the insured) either individually or as trustee, as beneficiary of his insurance. With respect to insurance which matured prior to 

August 1, 1946, the designated beneficiaries were restricted to the following permitted class: Widow, widower, child, parent, brother, or sister of the insured.

The insured since August 1, 1946, may select any one of the following four options for payment of insurance benefits upon his death: (1) Insurance payable in one lump sum; (2) insurance payable in equal monthly installments from 36 to 240 in number, in multiples of 12; (3) insurance payable for 120 months certain with such payments continuing during the remaining lifetime of the first beneficiary; (4) insurance payable as a refund life income in monthly installments payable for such period certain as may be required in order that the sum of the installments certain, including a last installment of such reduced amount as may be necessary, shall equal the face value of the contract, less any indebtedness, with such payments continuing throughout the lifetime of the first beneficiary: Provided, That such optional settlement shall not be available in any case in which such settlement would result in payments of installments over a shorter - period than 120 months. With respect to insurance which matured prior to August 1, 1946, settlement thereof is made only in monthly installments.

Unless the insured has elected some other mode of settlement the insurance maturing on or after August 1, 1946, is payable in 36 equal monthly installments. The first beneficiary may elect to receive payment under any option which provides for payment over a longer I period of time than the option elected by the insured, or if no option be designated by the insured in excess of 36 months which is a multiple of 12. Options 3 and 4 are not available if any firm, corporation, legal entity (including the estate of the insured), or trustee is beneficiary, or in any case in which an endowment contract matures by reason of the completion of the endowment period.

All permanent policies have guaranteed cash, loan, paid-up, and extended insurance values, if the policies have been in force for 1 year. 

National service life insurance is not assignable except that the person designated as beneficiary may assign all or any part of his interest in the insurance to the insured's widow, widower, child, father, mother, grandfather, grandmother, brother, or sister, provided the designated contingent beneficiary, if any, joins the beneficiary in the assignment and such assignment is delivered to the Veterans' Administration before any payments of the insurance have been made to the beneficiary.

Payments of national service life insurance, as such, are exempt from taxation, but such exemption does not extend to any property purchased therewith. Payments of insurance to a beneficiary under the provisions of the act are exempt from claims of creditors, and are not liable to attachment, levy, seizure by or under any legal or equitable process whatever, either before or after receipt by the beneficiary. 

National service life insurance which has lapsed because of failure to pay premiums may be reinstated at any time in accordance with existing regulations, payment of the required premiums, and submission of evidence of insurability satisfactory to the Administrator of 

Veterans' Affairs. 

Complete information pertaining to national service life insurance is contained in Veterans' Administration Pamphlet 9-3, "Information on National Service Life Insurance," which may be obtained from any branch office of the Veterans' Administration. For information as to the address of each branch office, see section 104, chapter I, of this manual.

Part B. United States Government Life (Converted) Insurance SECTION 303. GENERAL 

The insurance first provided for servicemen of World War I under the War Risk Insurance Act was yearly renewable term insurance for which the premium changed with age so long as the insurance was in effect. This form of insurance generally ceased on July 2, 1927, but prior to that date many veterans of World War I converted this term insurance into one of the other forms of insurance offered to them by the Government and designated as "United States Government life (converted) insurance" which includes the following kinds of policies: Ordinary life, 20-payment life, 30-payment life, 20-year endowment, 30-year endowment, endowment at age 62, and 5-year level-premium term insurance (38 U. S. C. 512). Only World War I 

veterans are eligible to apply for United States Government life insurance subsequent to October 8, 1940 (38 U. S. C. 511, 512a, 811).

SECTION 304. SPECIFIC PROVISIONS 

United States Government life (converted) insurance policies provide for the waiver of premium payments after the insured has incurred a disability which is total and permanent and also for the payment of monthly benefits to the insured, but these payments reduce the sum payable after the death of the insured to his surviving beneficiaries (38 U. S. C. 512a).

E 1 An additional provision granting benefits on account of total disability may be applied for by the insured upon the payment of an additional premium which is required for this provision. Monthly income payments and waiver of premiums are granted under this provision if the insured is totally disabled for a period of four consecutive months or more before attaining the age of 65. Such payments do not decrease the face value of the policy (38 U. S. C. 512b).

The insured, under a United States Government life (converted) insurance policy, may designate any person, firm, corporation, or legal entity as a beneficiary under his policy, either individually or as trustee.

The insured may select any one of the following four options for 

I payment of insurance benefits upon his death: Insurance payable in one lump sum; insurance payable in limited monthly installments (not less than 36 nor more than 240, in multiples of 12); insurance

I payable in continuous monthly installments throughout the lifetime of the beneficiary with 240 months certain; or insurance payable in continuous monthly installments throughout the lifetime of the bene- 

1 ficiary with 120 months certain (38 U. S. C. 512).

All permanent policies have guaranteed cash, loan, paid-up, and extended insurance values available if the policy has been in force 1 1 year. The 5-year level-premium term policy has no cash, loan, paid-up, or extended-insurance value (38 U.S. C. 512). 

United States Government life insurance, generally, is not assignable and the proceeds of a policy, except where invested in property, are exempt from taxation, and are not subject to the claims of private creditors of the insured or of the beneficiary, or of claims of the United States with certain exceptions. The insurance is not liable to attachment, levy, or seizure under any legal or equitable process either before or after receipt by the beneficiary (38 U. S. C. 454a).

United States Government life (converted) insurance which has lapsed because of failure to pay premiums may be reinstated at any time in accordance with existing regulations, payment of required premiums, and submission of evidence of insurability satisfactory to the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs (38 U. S. C. 515).

Complete information in reference to United States Government life insurance may be obtained by writing to the Veterans' Administration, Washington 25, D. C. Veterans' Administration Pamphlet 9–1, 

"Information and Premium Rates, United States Government Life 

Insurance" will be furnished by the Veterans' Administration upon request.

Part C. Guaranty of Premiums on Commercial Policies 1 SECTION 305. PROTECTION OF INSURANCE RIGHTS UNDER TERMS OF SOLDIERS' AND SAILORS' CIVIL RELIEF ACT OF 1940, AS AMENDED 

The United States, under the provisions of article IV, Soldiers' 1 and Sailors' Civil Relief Act of 1940, as amended, guarantees the payment of premiums of life insurance policies, for a total amount of insurance not to exceed $10,000, carried with private insurance companies by persons in the military or naval service of the United States (50 U. S. C. App. 540-548, inclusive).

Application for the protection of a policy may be made by the insured, or a person designated by him, or in case the insured is out-

side the continental United States, by a beneficiary. The original of the application should be sent to the insurer and a copy thereof to the 

Veterans' Administration. 

Any contract of life insurance in force on a premium-paying basis, on a life or endowment or term plan, and any benefit contract in the nature of life insurance arising out of membership in any fraternal or beneficial association, which was made and on which a premium was paid before October 6, 1942, or not less than 30 days before the insured entered the military service, is eligible for protection. A policy is not eligible for protection if it contains any provision excluding or restricting liability for death arising from or in connection with military service, or any other activity which the insured may be called upon to perform in connection with his military service, or if it requires the payment of an additional premium because of military service. 

No policy entitled to protection shall, subsequent to date of application, and during the period of military service of the insured, or during 2 years after expiration of such service, lapse or otherwise terminate or be forfeited by the nonpayment of a premium becoming due and payable, or the nonpayment of any indebtedness or interest. 

In the event of maturity of a policy as a death claim or otherwise before the expiration of the period of protection, the insurer in making settlement will deduct from the amount of insurance the premiums guaranteed, together with interest thereon at the rate fixed in the policy for policy loans. If no rate of interest is specifically fixed in the policy, the rate shall be the rate fixed for policy loans in other policies issued by the insurer at the time the protected policy was issued.

The payment of premiums and interest thereon at the rate fixed in the policy for policy loans while the policy is protected under the provisions of the Soldiers' and Sailors Civil Relief Act of 1940, as amended, is guaranteed by the United States and if the amount so guaranteed is not paid to the insurer prior to the expiration of the period of protection, the amount then due will be treated by the insurer as a loan on the policy. However, if at the expiration of the period of protection the cash surrender value of the policy is less than the amount guaranteed, the policy will cease and terminate and the United States will pay to the insurer the difference between the cash surrender value of the policy and the amount guaranteed. Any amount paid by the United States to an insurer on account of a protected policy will become a debt due to the United States by the insured on whose account payment was made, and such amount may be collected by deduction from any amount due the insured by the United States or as otherwise authorized by law. 

By virtue of section 4, Public Law 239, Eightieth Congress, July 25, 1947, the termination of World War II was fixed as of July 25, 1947, for the purposes of article IV as affected by the termination provisions of section 604 of the Soldiers' and Sailors' Civil Relief Act of 1940, as amended. Section 14 of the Selective Service Act of 1948, Public Law 

759, Eightieth Congress, June 24, 1948, provides that notwithstanding the provisions of section 604 of the Soldiers' and Sailors' Civil Relief Act of 1940, and the provisions of section 4, Public Law 239, Eightieth Congress, all of the provisions of the Soldiers' and Sailors'

Civil Relief Act of 1940, as amended, including specifically article IV thereof, shall be applicable to all persons in the armed forces of the 

United States.

Part A. Introduction 

51 

SECTION 401. INTRODUCTION 

Prior to the enactment of the Economy Act of March 20, 1933 (Public, No. 2, 73d Cong.; 38 U. S. C. 701–721), monetary benefits were payable under the general pension law for service-connected dis- I ability or death, based upon service subsequent to March 4, 1861, excepting World War I service. Other benefits, known as service 21

pensions, were payable to certain veterans and dependents of deceased veterans of the various wars prior to World War I, based upon attained age, non-service-connected disability, or death.

The World War Veterans' Act, 1924, as amended, provided for payment of compensation for death or disability directly or presumptively incurred in or aggravated by military or naval service in World War I on or after April 6, 1917, and before July 2, 1921, and of disability allowance for permanent disabilities partial or total in degree which were not due to service. Provision was also made for payment of compensation for disability or death resulting from injuries received in connection with training, hospitalization, medical or surgical treatment, etc.

Retirement benefits were also provided for officers and enlisted men of the Regular Army, Navy, Marine Corps, or Coast Guard based upon length of service, disability, or age, including benefits for emergency officers of the Army, Navy, and Marine Corps during World 

War I, retired for disability.

Under Public Law 494, Seventy-ninth Congress, July 9, 1946 (38 

U. S. C. 700), a distinction is made in the terminology applied to benefits (other than retirement pay) which are payable on account of service-connected disability or death and those based on non-serviceconnected disability or death. The former are designated as "compensation," and not "pension."

Section 17 of the act of March 20, 1933, subject to certain saving provisions, repealed all public laws granting medical or hospital treatment, domiciliary care, compensation and other allowances, pension, disability allowance, or retirement pay to veterans and the dependents of veterans of the Spanish-American War, Philippine Insurrection, Boxer Rebellion, and World War I or to former members of the military or naval service for injury or disease incurred or aggravated in line of duty in the military or naval service, except so far as they related to persons who served prior to the Spanish-American War and the dependents of such persons and to the retirement of officers and enlisted men of the Regular Army, Navy, Marine Corps, or Coast Guard. This act and Veterans Regulations promulgated thereunder provided a new system of relief for veterans and the dependents of deceased veterans whose rights to monetary and other benefits were affected by the laws thus repealed. 

Part I of Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended, promulgated pursuant to the act of March 20, 1933, provides for payment of compensation at wartime rates for death or disability, not the result of misconduct, incurred or aggravated in line of duty in active military or naval service during the Spanish-American War, Philippine Insur rection, Boxer Rebellion, World War I, or World War II as service during these war periods is defined therein. Compensation is also provided at peacetime rates, which are 80 percent of the wartime rates, for death or disability, not the result of misconduct, incurred or aggravated in line of duty in active military or naval service on or after April 21, 1898, other than in a period of war service as defined therein. 

Where certain service requirements are met, and subject to an income limitation, pensions are also provided for veterans of World War I and World War II for non-service-connected permanent and total disabilities not the result of the veteran's own willful misconduct or vicious habits.

3 While the act of March 20, 1933, repealed the law granting emergency officers' retirement pay, it provided for continuation of such retirement pay under certain conditions. The act of July 15, 1940 (Public, No. 743, 76th Cong.; 38 U. S. C. 582 a, b), modified these conditions and authorized payment of such benefits to those emergency officers who failed to file claim within the time limit specified in any case where the disability resulted from injury or disease incurred in i combat with an enemy of the United States. The act of July 18, 1940 (Public, No. 746, 76th Cong.; 38 U. S. C. 582c), provided for the granting of emergency officers' retirement benefits to World War I provisional, probationary, or temporary officers of the military or naval forces or Coast Guard who served subsequent to April 6, 1917, and who were then in a status of honorable separation from service and who otherwise met the requirements of the acts of March 20, 1933, and July 15, 1940. Disability retirement pay provided for certain 

Reserve officers, Army of the United States, under the act of September 26, 1941 (Public Law 262, 77th Cong.; 10 U. S. C. 456a; 38 U.S. C. 

12), and for officers, Army of the United States, under the act of April 3, 1939, as amended (10 U. S. C. 456), is payable by the Veterans' Administration, although eligibility therefor is determined by the service department.

Under the act of March 20, 1933, and the Veterans Regulations, as amended, World War I is considered to have ended with the cessation of hostilities on November 11, 1918, or April 1, 1920, if there was service in Russia. However, except with respect to emergency officers' retirement pay, service during a reenlistment in the military or naval service on or after November 12, 1918, and before July 2, 1921, where there was prior service between April 6, 1917, and November 11, 1918, is considered World War I service. 

Veterans of World War I who enlisted on or after November 12, 1918, and had no prior service between April 6, 1917, and November 11, 1918, and their dependents are not entitled to wartime rates, except under the provisions of paragraph I (c), part II, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended, hereinafter discussed, but are entitled to rates authorized for peacetime service.

Veterans of World War II, originally, were not entitled to wartime rates under the act of March 20, 1933, and the Veterans Regulations, as service in World War II was not defined therein. However, benefits at peacetime rates, in accordance with the eligibility requirements for peacetime service, were payable in such cases. Upon the enactment of Public Law 359, Seventy-seventh Congress, December 19, 1941, wartime rates were provided for veterans and the dependents of vet erans entitled to benefits under part II, Veterans Regulation No. 1 

(a), as amended, or the general pension law for service-connected disability or death if the disability or death resulted from an injury or disease received in line of duty (1) as a direct result of armed conflict, or (2) while engaged in extrahazardous service, including such service under conditions simulating war, or (3) while the United States is engaged in war. Pursuant to this law, veterans of World 

War II became eligible for benefits at wartime rates, but were still governed by the eligibility requirements applicable to peacetime service. Under section 9 (a) of Public Law 144, Seventy-eighth Con gress, as limited by Presidential Proclamation No. 2714, December 31, 1946, and Public Law 239, Eightieth Congress, Veterans Regulation

No. 1 (a), as amended, is applicable in determination of entitlement to death or disability compensation and allows wartime rates in cases where there was service in an enlistment or employment entered into during the period December 7, 1941, to December 31, 1946, and the death or disability occurred in or resulted from injuries or aggravation of preexisting injuries in active service during the period beginning 

December 7, 1941, and ending at midnight on July 25, 1947. 

Additional compensation was provided by Public Law 877, Eightieth 

Congress, July 2, 1948, for persons entitled to wartime rates of compensation under part I or paragraph I (c), part II, of Veterans Regu lation No. 1 (a), as amended, or the World War Veterans' Act, 1924, as amended, whose disability is rated 60 percent or greater and who have a wife, child, children, or dependent parents. This law likewise provided for additional compensation in similar cases of veterans entitled to peacetime rates under paragraph II, part II, Veterans Regu lation No. 1 (a), as amended.

The monthly rates of compensation payable to veterans of World 

War I and World War II, including veterans entitled to wartime rates under Public Law 359, Seventy-seventh Congress, December 19, 1941, for service on or after September 16, 1940, for service-incurred disability, not including the special awards and allowances fixed by law which are payable under any laws or regulations administered by the Veterans' Administration, were increased by 15 percent, effective June 1, 1944 (Public Law 312, 78th Cong., May 27, 1944).

Shortly thereafter, this 15-percent increase in the monthly rates for service-incurred disability, exclusive of special awards and allowances fixed by law, was extended by Public Law 469, Seventy-eighth Congress, December 7, 1944, to veterans not included in the groups covered by Public Law 312, Seventy-eighth Congress, supra, and as a result veterans of all wars and of the regular peace establishment are entitled to this increase in the monthly rates for service-incurred disability effective June 1, 1944. 

Rates of compensation and pension payable to veterans of World

War I and World War II and their dependents were increased 20 percent by section 2, Public Law 662, Seventy-ninth Congress, August 8, 1946 (38 U. S. C., ch. 12). Compensation rates payable to dependents of such veterans were further increased by Public Law 868, Eightieth 

Congress, July 1, 1948. Peacetime compensation rates payable to veterans were increased by section 1, Public Law 659, Seventy-ninth 

Congress, August 8, 1946 (38 U. S. C., ch 12). Public Law 876,

Eightieth Congress, July 2, 1948, further increased peacetime compensation rates payable under paragraph I (a), part II, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended, by providing that such rates be raised to 

80 percent of wartime compensation rates. Public Law 868, Eightieth 

Congress, July 1, 1948, similarly raised peacetime compensation rates payable to dependents under paragraph III, part II, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended, to 80 percent of wartime compensation rates.

Under paragraph I (c), part II, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended by Public Law 359, Seventy-seventh Congress and by section 2 of Public Law 868, Eightieth Congress, any veteran entitled to benefits under part II of Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended, or the general pension law, for service-connected disability, is entitled

to the wartime rates prescribed in part I of said regulation, as amended, where the conditions of the statute are met and the dependents of any deceased veteran whose death resulted from injury or disease received in line of duty under such conditions, otherwise en titled to benefits under part II of Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended, or the general pension law, are entitled to compensation at the rates provided for service-connected death benefits for dependents of World War I and World War II veterans. As to dependents, other than the dependent mother or father of the deceased veteran, these rates were increased by section 14 (a), Public Law 144, Seventy-eighth Congress, July 13, 1943. The current compensation rates payable to dependents of veterans of World War I and World War II which were established by Public Law 868, Eightieth Congress, July 1, 1948, are set out in section 404. 

Public, No. 141, Seventy-third Congress, March 28, 1934, reenacted, with limitations, many of the provisions of the World War Veterans Act, 1924, as amended, including the provision authorizing payment of compensation for injury or death as the result of training, hospitalization, medical or surgical treatment, etc. With respect to veterans of World War I in receipt, except by fraud, mistake, or misrepresentation, of compensation on March 19, 1933, for loss of use of both eyes, section 26 of the act of March 28, 1934, provided that such compensation should not be reduced or discontinued, except in accordance with regulations pertaining to hospitalized cases. This provision was later modified by Public, No. 196, Seventy-sixth Congress, July 19, 1939, as to veterans of World War I suffering from certain misconduct disabilities in receipt of compensation therefor on March 19, 1933, and their dependents. Section 7 of Public, No. 866, Seventy-sixth 

Congress, approved October 17, 1940, however, removed the requirement that the veteran must have been on the rolls March 19, 1933, and provided for death benefits under Public, No. 484, Seventy-third Congress, June 28, 1934, as amended, for the dependents of those comprehended by this provision. The Act of March 28, 1934, restored to the rolls those veterans of World War I who were in receipt of compensation under the World War Veterans' Act, 1924, as amended, for disabilities directly or presumptively connected with service under that act and who had been removed from the rolls by the enactment of 

Public, No. 2, Seventy-third Congress, March 20, 1933, provided they could meet the eligibility requirements of the act of March. 28, 1934, and authorized payment of compensation to veterans of World War I not on the rolls on March 19, 1933, who are able to meet the eligibility requirements of that act.

The foregoing statutory enactments and the Veterans Regulations, as amended or modified by later statutes, are the basic laws administered by the Veterans' Administration under which monetary benefits (compensation, pensions, retirement pay) are payable to veterans and the dependents of deceased veterans of World War I, World War II, and the Regular Establishment. The different groups of veterans or their dependents may be entitled to monetary benefits for serviceconnected or non-service-connected disability or death under various laws administered by the Veterans' Administration. Where the claimant is entitled to monetary benefits under more than one provision of law and regulations, he will be awarded and paid the greater benefit. 81200°-48- -3

Part B. Benefits Based on Service in World War II SECTION 402. COMPENSATION TO VETERANS OF WORLD WAR II FOR SERVICE-CONNECTED DISABILITY (A) GENERAL Compensation is payable under Public, No. 2, Seventy-third Con gress, March 20, 1933, and the Veterans Regulations as modified or amended, to veterans of World War II for disability resulting from injury or disease incurred or aggravated in line of duty, not the result of misconduct, in an enlistment or employment entered into during the period December 7, 1941, to December 31, 1946, where the dis ability occurred in active service during the period beginning December 7, 1941, and ending July 25, 1947 (par. I (a), pt. I, Veterans 

Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended by sec. 9 (a), Public Law 144, 78th 

Cong., July 13, 1943, 38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations, Presi dential Proclamation No. 2714, Dec. 31, 1946, Public Law 239, 80th

Congress). The wartime rates are also payable pursuant to paragraph 

I (c), part II, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended, where the disability resulted from disease or injuries incurred in or aggravated by service during an enlistment or employment entered into subsequent to December 31, 1946, and which occurred on or prior to July 25, 1947, but service connection and other criteria governing entitlement in such cases are those provided in part II for peacetime cases. 

The war rates range from $13.80 to $138 per month, with special rates or allowances for specific disabilities ranging as high as $360 per month. They include a 15-percent increase (except as to special awards and allowances fixed by law) authorized effective June 1, 1944, and an additional 20-percent increase authorized effective September 

1, 1946 (pt. I, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended; Public 

Law 312, 78th Cong., May 27, 1944; sec. 2, Public Law 662, 79th Cong., August 8, 1946; 38 U. S. C. 471a-1, ch. 12, Veterans Regulations). 

By a recent enactment (Public Law 877, 80th Cong., July 2, 1948) additional compensation was provided for those with dependents, where the disability is rated at not less than 60 percent, in amounts ranging from $17.50 additional per month, for one dependent parent where the veteran's disability is rated total to $56 additional per month for a wife and three or more children where the disability is rated total. If the disability is rated less than total but not less than 60 percent the amount of the additional compensation for dependents is in an amount having the same ratio to the amount applicable to a rating of total disability as the degree of the partial disability bears to total disbility. 

(B) PRESUMPTION OF SOUND CONDITION 

Every person employed in the active military or naval service during 

World War II as defined in part I, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended, shall be taken to have been in sound condition when examined, accepted, and enrolled for service except as to defects, infirmi ties, or disorders noted at time of examination, acceptance, and enrollment, or where clear and unmistakable evidence demonstrates that the injury or disease existed prior to acceptance and enrollment and was not aggravated by such active military or naval service (par. I (b),

pt. I, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended by sec. 9 (b), Public Law 144, 78th Cong., July 13, 1943; 38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations). 

(C) PRESUMPTION AS TO SERVICE CONNECTION 

A chronic disease becoming manifest to a degree of 10 percent or more within 1 year from date of separation from active service in World War II, as such service is defined in part I, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended, shall be considered to have been incurred. in or aggravated by service notwithstanding there is no record of evidence of such disease during the period of active service, provided the person suffering from such disease served 90 days or more in the active service. However, where there is affirmative evidence to the contrary or evidence to establish that an intercurrent injury or disease which is a recognized cause of such chronic disease has been suffered between the date of discharge and the onset of the chronic disease, or the disability is due to the person's own misconduct, service connection will not be in order (par. I (c), pt. I, Veterans Regulation No. 

1 (a); 38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations). By Public Law 

748, Eightieth Congress, approved June 24, 1948, this provision was amended to provide that "chronic disease" shall include certain specified diseases and such others as the Administrator may add to the list; also to provide that subject to the limitations of the regulation (par. 

I (c) above) tropical diseases such as those named in Public Law 748 and others, and the resultant disorders or diseases originating because of therapy, administered in connection with such diseases, or as a preventative thereof, shall be accorded service connection when shown. to exist, to a degree of 10 percent or more, within 1 year after separation from active service, or at a time when standard and accepted treatises indicate that the incubation period thereof commenced during active service. It was further provided that nothing in paragraph 

I (c) shall be construed to prevent service connection for any disease or disorder otherwise shown by sound judgment to have been incurred in or aggravated by active service. 

(D) MISCONDUCT 

An injury or disease incurred during military or naval service will be deemed to have been incurred in line of duty and not the result of the veteran's own misconduct when the person on whose account benefits are claimed was, at the time the injury was suffered or disease contracted, in active service in the military or naval forces, whether on active duty or on authorized leave, unless such injury or disease was the result of his own willful misconduct. Venereal disease is not presumed to be due to willful misconduct if the person in service complies with the Army or Navy regulations requiring him to report and receive treatment for such disease. The requirement for line of duty will not be met if it appears that at the time the injury was suffered or disease contracted the person on whose account benefits are claimed (1) was avoiding duty by deserting the service, or by absenting himself without leave materially interfering with the performance of military duties; (2) was confined under sentence of court martial or civil court (par. VIII, Veterans Regulation No. 10, as amended by Public

Law 439, 78th Cong., September 27, 1944; 38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations). 

(E) CHARACTER OF DISCHARGE 

A discharge or release from active service under conditions other than dishonorable is a prerequisite to entitlement to veterans' benefits (sec. 1503, Public Law 346, 78th Cong., June 22, 1944; 38 U. S. C. 697c).

The discharge or dismissal by reason of the sentence of a general court martial of any person from the military or naval forces, or the discharge of any such person on the ground that he was a conscientious objector who refused to perform military duty or refused to wear the uniform or otherwise to comply with lawful orders of competent military authority, or as a deserter, or of an officer by the acceptance of his resignation for the good of the service, shall bar all rights of such person, based upon the period of service from which he is so discharged or dismissed, under any laws administered by the Veterans' Administration. If at the time of the commission of such an offense the person was insane, he is not precluded from benefits to which he is otherwise entitled under the laws administered by the Veterans' Administration. These provisions are not applicable with respect to any war risk, Government (converted) or national service life-insurance policy (sec. 300, Public Law 346, 78th Cong., June 22, 1944; 38 U. S. C. 693g). 

(F) STATEMENTS AGAINST INTEREST 

No person in the armed forces shall be required to sign a statement of any nature relating to the origin, incurrence, or aggravation of any disease or injury he may have, and any such statement against his own interest signed at any time, shall be null and void and of no force and effect (sec. 105, Public Law 346, 78th Cong., June 22, 1944, 38 U. S. C. 693e).

SECTION 403. PENSIONS TO VETERANS OF WORLD WAR II FOR NON- SERVICE-CONNECTED DISABILITY (A) ELIGIBILITY REQUIREMENTS 

Any veteran who served in the active military or naval service during World War II, for a period of 90 days or more, and who was discharged therefrom under conditions other than dishonorable and under conditions other than those specified in section 300 of the Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944, set forth in section 402 (E), or who, having served less than 90 days, was discharged for disability incurred in the service in line of duty, who is shown to have been in active service therein before noon of December 31, 1946, may be entitled to receive a pension under part III, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended, for permanent total disability not the result of his own willful misconduct or vicious habits and which is not shown to have been incurred in any period of military or naval service. The period of service applicable to veterans of World War II extends from, on, or after December 7, 1941, to noon of December 31, 1946 (sec. 9 (a), Public Law 144, 78th Cong., July 13, 1943; 38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Vet-

rans Regulations; Presidential Proclamation 2714, December 31, .946).

In determining the period of service, it is not requisite that the 90 lays' period of service shall have been completed before the cessation of hostilities. It is necessary, however, that the veteran shall have entered service not later than December 31, 1946, and shall have Served continuously thereafter for 90 days. A period of continuous ɩctive service for 90 days which commenced prior to and extended Into the period beginning December 7, 1941, will meet the service requirements of part III, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended. 

(B) RATES OF BENEFIT 

The rate of pension payable in such cases is $60 monthly, except hat where the veteran shall have been rated permanent and total and n receipt of pension for a continuous period of 10 years or reaches he age of 65 years and is permanently and totally disabled, the amount of pension is $72 monthly. However, this pension is not payable if the veteran's income, if he is unmarried, exceeds $1,000, or, if he is married, or has minor children, exceeds $2,500. In determining annual income, payments of war-risk term insurance, United States Government life (converted) insurance, national service life insurance, payments under the World War Adjusted Compensation Act, as amended, the Adjusted Compensation Payment Act, 1936, and amounts received as overtime compensation or additional compensation for employment in Federal Government service may not be considered (pt. III, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended by sec. 

403, Public Law 844, 74th Cong., June 29, 1936; sec. 12, Public Law 

49, 78th Cong., May 7, 1943; Public Law 313, 78th Cong., May 27, 1944; sec. 2, Public Law 662, 79th Cong., Aug. 8, 1946; 38 U. Š. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations; 50 U. S. C. App. 1412).

SECTION 404. COMPENSATION TO DEPENDENTS OF VETERANS OF WORLD WAR II FOR SERVICE-CONNECTED DEATH 

The surviving widow, child or children, and dependent parents of any deceased person, whose death resulted from disease or injury not the result of misconduct, incurred in or aggravated in line of duty in the active military or naval forces on or after December 7, 1941, and prior to July 26, 1947, may be entitled to death compensation under 

Public No. 2, Seventy-third Congress, March 20, 1933, and the Veterans Regulations, as amended (Public Law 868, 80th Cong., July 1, 1948).

The term "widow of a World War II veteran" means a woman who was married to the veteran prior to noon, December 31, 1956 (par. 

V, Veterans Regulation No. 10, as amended by sec. 6, Public Law 144, 78th Cong., July 13, 1943, 38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations;

Presidential Proclamation No. 2714, December 31, 1946). Compen sation payable to the widow continues until her death or remarriage, and where properly discontinued by reason of remarriage it may not thereafter be recommenced (par. IV (a), pt. I, Veterans Regulation 

No. 2 (a); 38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations).

The term "child" means a person unmarried and under the age of 18 years, unless prior to reaching the age of 18 years the child becomes

or has become permanently incapable of self-support by reason of mental or physical defect, who is a legitimate child; a child legally adopted; a stepchild if a member of the man's household; an illegiti mate child but as to the father only if acknowledged in writing, signed by him, or if he has been judicially ordered or decreed to contribute to the child's support or has been, prior to his death judicially decreed to be the putative father of such child, or if he is otherwise shown by evidence satisfactory to the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs to be the putative father of such child. Payment of compensation will be continued after the eighteenth birthday and until completion of edu cation or training (but not after such child reaches the age of 21 years) any child who is or may hereafter be pursuing a course of instruction at a school, college, academy, seminary, technical institute, or univer sity, particularly designated by him and approved by the Administrator, which shall have agreed to report to the Administrator the termi nation of attendance of such child, and if any such institution of learning fails to make such report promptly the approval will be with drawn (par. VI, Veterans Regulation No. 10 series, as amended by sec. 7, Public Law 144, 78th Cong., July 13, 1943, 38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations). to 

The terms "parent," "mother," and "father" include the mother or father of the veteran, the mother or father of the veteran through legal adoption, and persons who have stood in loco parentis to the veteran at any time prior to his entry into active service for a period of not less than 1 year. Not more than one father and one mother, as defined, will be recognized in any case, and preference will be given to such father or mother who actually exercised parental relationship at the time of, or most nearly prior to, the date of entry into active service by the veteran (par. VII, Veterans Regulation No. 10, as amended by sec. 8, Public Law 144, 78th Cong., July 13, 1943, 38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations).

Parents must furnish proof of actual dependency. However, there is no time limit as to when the dependency of a parent must begin, and death compensation is payable to parents otherwise entitled, when de pendency arises, regardless of the date of the death of the veteran. 

Remarriage of a dependent mother or father will not terminate payments, where dependency otherwise exists. (Public Law 193, 77th 

Cong., July 30, 1941, 38 U. S. C. 725). Inasmuch as the right to dependents compensation benefits is based upon relationship, proof thereof must be made in a manner satisfactory to the Veterans' Administration. Proof of service connection may be established after death occurs. The monthly rates of compensation payable to depend ents of both World War I and World War II veterans are as follows: 

Widow, with no child_._ 

Widow, with 1 child (with $15 for each additional child).

No widow, 1 child___.
