Service, presently the Federal Security Administrator. The Board is charged with the determination of all matters of policy relating to the administration of the Veterans' Employment Service of the United 

States Employment Service. All Federal agencies are required to furnish the Board such records, statistics, or information as may be deemed necessary or appropriate, and otherwise to cooperate with the Board in providing continuous employment opportunities for veterans. 

The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs as Chairman of the Vet erans' Placement Service Board has direct authority and responsibility for carrying out its policies through the veterans' employment representatives in the several States and is authorized to delegate such authority to an executive secretary appointed by him who also serves as Chief of the Veterans' Employment Service of the United States Employment Service. 

The term "veteran" as used in title IV of the Servicemen's Read justment Act (38 U. S. C. 695f) means a person who served in the active service of the armed forces during a period of war in which the United States has been, or is, engaged and who has been discharged or released therefrom under conditions other than dishonorable. 

A person who served in the armed forces of a government allied with the United States in World War II and who at time of entrance into such service was a citizen of the United States, and who at time of application is also a resident of the United States, is eligible for the benefits of title IV of the Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944, as amended, under the same conditions as set forth above (38 U. S. C. 697f).

SECTION 767. VETERANS' EMPLOYMENT SERVICE 

The United States Employment Service which is now a part of the Federal Security Agency was established under authority of the Wagner-Peyser Act of June 6, 1933 (29 U. S. C. 49–49 (1)), to promote and develop a national system of public employment offices; to maintain a veterans' service to be devoted to securing employment for veterans; to maintain a farm placement service; to maintain a public employment service for the District of Columbia; and to assist in establishing and maintaining systems of public employment offices in the several States and political subdivisions thereof in which there shall be located a veterans' employment service. The United States Employment 

Service comprises a Nation-wide network of permanent and itinerant offices located throughout the United States and Territories all of which in effect are veterans' employment offices. 

Effective November 16, 1946, the operation of the State and local employment offices, which in 1942 were brought directly under Federal control to promote the war effort, was transferred back to the State agencies designated to administer the State-wide system of public employment offices in cooperation with the United States Employment Service. The Veterans' Employment Service was not affected by this transfer and continues to function through the State veterans' employment representatives under the supervision and control of the head quarters of the Veterans' Employment Service, United States Employment Service, Federal Security Agency.

Veterans' employment representatives.-The United States Em ployment Service is required under section 601 of the Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944, as amended (38 U. S. C. 695a), to assign to each of the States a veterans' employment representative who shall be a veteran of the wars of the United States separated from active service under honorable conditions, who at the time of appointment shall have been a bona fide resident of the State for at least 2 years, and who shall be appointed, subject to the approval of the Veterans' Placement Service Board, in accordance with civil-service laws, and whose compensation shall be fixed in accordance with the Classification 

Act of 1923, as amended (5 U. S. C. 661–684).

Each veterans' employment representative, and his assistants, attached to the staff of the public employment service in the State to which they have been assigned are administratively responsible to the 

Veterans' Placement Service Board, through its executive secretary (the Chief of the Veterans' Employment Service), for the execution of the Board's veterans' placement policies. In cooperation with and working through the public employment service in the State, the veterans' employment representative— (a) is functionally responsible for the supervision of the registration of veterans in local employment offices for suitable types of employment and for placement of veterans in employment; (b) assists in securing and maintaining current information as to the various types of available employment in public works and private industry or business; (c) promotes the interest of employers in employing veterans; (d) maintains regular contact with employers and veterans' organizations with a view of keeping employers advised of veterans available for employment and veterans advised of opportunities for employment; and (e) assists in every possible way in improving working conditions and the advancement of employment of veterans. 

In addition to the State veterans' employment representative who coordinates the Federal, State, and local employment assistance programs for veterans, there is assigned to each local public employment office a veterans' employment representative, administratively responsible to the local manager, who works with and carries out the policies of the Veterans' Employment Service. 

The local veterans' employment representative acts as job counselor and employment placement officer, advising and assisting veterans, including those with partial disabilities, in finding suitable employment, both public and private. In carrying out the duties prescribed the local veterans' representative cooperates with all Federal and State agencies and private organizations in developing training and employment opportunities for veterans.

In every local public employment office it is the policy as estab lished by the Veterans' Placement Service Board and the Veterans' 

Employment Service to refer qualified applicants for employment in the following order of preference: 

1. Disabled war veterans. 

2. Other war veterans. 

3. Nonveterans.

Every unemployed veteran should register with the nearest local public employment office and remain actively registered until he finds suitable employment. The address of the local public employment office may be obtained by consulting the telephone directory, the veterans' information center in the community, the Selective Service local board, or the nearest office of the Veterans' Administration. 

Veterans who are unable to obtain the assistance they desire at their local public employment office should communicate with the veterans' employment representative for their State. The following is a list of the State veterans' employment representatives:

SECTION 768. RECRUITMENT AND PLACEMENT IN FEDERAL EMPLOY- MENT, UNITED STATES CIVIL SERVICE COMMISSION 

The United States Civil Service Commission, created by the act of 

January 16, 1883 (5 U. S. C. 632-633, 635) recruits qualified persons to fill permanent positions in the Federal service through the public announcement of examinations to test the fitness of applicants; passes on the qualifications of applicants and on veterans' preference claims (see pt. H. Veterans' Preference in Federal Civilian Employment); passes on any questions as to reinstatement of returning veterans (see pt. G. Reemployment of Veterans); and formulates policies and procedures for the maximum utilization of veterans, coordinating its own activities with those of other Federal departments and agencies, veterans organizations, and groups interested in rehabilitation and employment of veterans.

Čivil-service examinations which lead to permanent appointment are announced through such means as notices posted on bulletin boards in first- and second-class post offices; notices distributed to schools, public libraries, and organizations; and through news items in the press. Application forms may be obtained from first- and secondclass post offices, from the United States Civil Service Commission, Washington 25, D. C., or from any of the Commission's 14 regional offices. An application for an examination should be sent to the office of the Civil Service Commission announcing the examination. After an examination is held, those who qualify are placed on lists of eligibles from which appointments are made to permanent positions in the Federal civil service. Applications for temporary appointment should be sent direct to the Federal agency in which employment is desired.

SECTION 769. OPERATION OF VENDING STANDS BY THE BLIND, OFFICE OF EDUCATION, FEDERAL SECURITY AGENCY The Office of Education, Federal Security Agency, administers the act of June 20, 1936 (20 U. S. C. 107–107f), under which Federal funds are made available to assist blind persons to obtain remunerative employment through the operation of vending stands in Federal and other buildings in the United States. The State blind commission or other State public agency designated by the Office of Education to issue licenses to blind persons eligible for such employment cooperates with the Office of Education and with the division of vocational rehabilitation of the State in training, placing, and supervising blind persons and provides through loan, gift, or otherwise, an adequate initial stock of suitable articles to be vended for each blind person licensed to operate a stand.

In branch-office and field-station buildings controlled by the Veterans' Administration, space for the operation of vending stands for

the sale of cigarettes, tobacco, candies, magazines and other periodicals will be made available without a rental charge where need for an operation of such stand is approved by the Deputy Administrator. 

In every case preference will be given a blind veteran.

Part G. Reemployment of Veterans SECTION 770. VETERANS' REEMPLOYMENT RIGHTS Section 8 of the Selective Training and Service Act of 1940, as amended; section 3 of the Army Reserve and Retired Personnel Service 

Law of 1940, as amended; section 7 of the Service Extension Act of 

1941, as amended; section 212 of the Public Health Service Act; and section 9 of the Selective Service Act of 1948, confer reemployment rights upon persons who shall have left their positions to, and in fact did, enter active military or naval service within the meaning of these statutes. This legislation was designed to aid the veteran in his readjustment to civilian life by overcoming insofar as possible the handicap resulting from prolonged absence, giving him, among other employment benefits, an opportunity to reacquire skills he may have lost due to absence.

SECTION 771. PERSONS TO WHOM REEMPLOYMENT PROVISIONS APPLY (A) Under acts prior to the Selective Service Act of 1948: 

(a) Any person inducted for military training and service in the land and naval forces of the United States on or after September 16, 1940, under the provisions of the Selective Training and Service Act of 1940, as amended (50 App. U. S. C. 308). (b) Any member of any reserve component of the land or naval forces of the United States who was on active duty on August 27, 1940, or who was called to active duty after that date (50 App. U. S. C. 403). (c) Any person who, subsequent to May 1, 1940, entered upon active military or naval service in the land or naval forces of the United 

States (50 App. U. S. C. 357). 

(d) Any Reserve officers of the United States Public Health Service called to active duty after November 11, 1943 (42 U. S. C. 213). (e) Any former members of Women's Army Auxiliary Corps who, within 90 days after termination of service in that corps, entered active military service in Women's Army Corps, with respect to a civilian position left to perform service in WAAC (50 App. U.S.C. 357). 

(B) Under the Selective Service Act of 1948 (Public Law 759, 80th 

Cong., June 24, 1948): (a) Any person inducted into the armed forces for training and service under this act. (b) Any person who, for the first time after the date of enactment of this act (June 24, 1948) and while it is in effect, enlists in the armed forces of the United States or the Coast Guard (other than in a reserve component) for not more than 3 years. (c) Any person who, after the date of enactment of this act and while it is in effect, enters upon active duty in the armed forces of the 

United States, the Coast Guard or the Public Health Service in re sponse to an order or call to active duty, if relieved from active duty not later than 3 years after entering on active duty or as soon thereafter as he is able to obtain orders relieving him from active duty.

SECTION 772. CONDITIONS OF REINSTATEMENT 

Any person coming under the provisions of the statutes set forth above who has served honorably in the armed forces of the United States is entitled under the law, if still qualified to perform the duties of his former position, to reinstatement in his former position or to a position of like seniority, status, and pay, under the following conditions: (a) If such position, other than a temporary position, was in the employ of a private employer, unless the employer's circumstances have so changed as to make it impossible or unreasonable to restore the veteran to his former position, or to a position of like seniority, status, and pay. The Selective Service Act of 1948 specifically imposes this requirement on the former employer's "successor in interest"; many courts had reached the same result under the previous laws. (b) If such position, other than a temporary position, was in the employ of the United States Government, its Territories or possessions, or the District of Columbia, restoration is mandatory. The Selective 

Service Act of 1948 (sec. 9 (e) (2) and (3)) specifically includes positions in the legislative and judicial branches of the Government. 

The proviso that an employer is not obligated to reinstate a veteran in his former position, or in a position of like seniority, status, and pay, in the event his circumstances have so changed as to make it impossible or unreasonable for him to do so, cannot be invoked as to employees of the United States, its Territories or possessions, or the District of Columbia.

As to positions in the employ of any State or political subdivision thereof, it is declared to be the sense of the Congress that such person should be restored to such position or to a position of like seniority, status, and pay. (c) If he left such position in order to enter upon active military or naval service in the land or naval forces of the United States. (d) If he satisfactorily completed his period of training and service or period of active duty and received a certificate to that effect. (e) If he makes application for reemployment within 90 days after he is relieved from military training and service or from hospitalization continuing after discharge for a period of not more than 1 year. 

Employees disabled in service.-Under the Selective Service Act of 

1948, an ex-serviceman who is not qualified to perform the duties of his former position by reason of disability sustained in service but qualified to perform the duties of any other position in the employ of the employer, shall be restored to such other position the duties of which he is qualified to perform as will provide him like seniority, status, and pay, or the nearest approximation thereof consistent with the circumstances in his case. This requirement applies to both private employers and to agencies of the United States Government, its Territories, possessions, political subdivisions, and the District of Columbia.

SECTION 773. APPLICATION FOR REEMPLOYMENT 

Private employees.-Request for restoration to previous employment must be made within the prescribed period of 90 days after release from service or from hospitalization.

The veteran should also be prepared to submit evidence of the satisfactory completion of his period of training and service or period of active duty by submission of his certificate of discharge or transfer to inactive status, or other evidence of separation from and satisfactory completion of service, including a record of any special proficiency or merit attained.

If the veteran needs advice as to his rights or assistance in filing his application he should consult the local employment office of his State employment service or the field office of the Bureau of Veterans' Reemployment Rights in his area (see sec. 776 for list of field offices). 

Federal employees.-Application for restoration to Federal employment should be made to the agency which the veteran left when he entered the armed forces or to the agency which has taken over the functions of his former agency. If the agency and its functions have been entirely abolished, or if for any reason the veteran is unable to secure employment, he should apply to the nearest regional office of the United States Civil Service Commission or direct to the Civil Service Commission, Washington, D. C. He may seek information and assistance concerning his reemployment rights from the Bureau of Veterans' Reemployment Rights, Department of Labor. The Civil 

Service Commission, through its Veterans' Service Section, has primary responsibility for administering the reemployment rights of veterans in Federal service. Inquiries for information may be made to the United States Civil Service Commission (Veterans' Service Sec tion), Washington, D. C., or in the field to the appropriate representative or office. (See sec. 768 for list of regional offices.)

The veteran must apply for restoration to duty not later than 90 days after release from military service, or from hospitalization which continued for a period of not more than 1 year.

Section 9 (e) (1) of the Selective Service Act of 1948 requires res toration of a former employee by any agency in the executive branch of the Government or by any Territory or possession, or political subdivision thereof, or by the District of Columbia. If an agency in the executive branch no longer exists and its functions have not been transferred to another agency or if, for any reason, it is not feasible for such person to be restored to employment by such agency or the District of Columbia the Civil Service Commission is required to determine whether there is a position in any other agency in the executive branch of the Government or in the government of the District of Columbia for which such person is qualified which is vacant or held by a person having a temporary appointment thereto. If there is such position the veteran shall be restored to it. If any agency in the executive branch of the Government or the District of Columbia fails or refuses to comply with the provisions of this section, the Civil Service Commission is authorized and directed to issue an order requiring compliance with such provisions and compensation to such person for any loss of salary or wages suffered by reason of failure to comply, less any amounts received by him through other employment, unemployment compensation, or readjustment allowances.

SECTION 774. RIGHTS AFTER REINSTATEMENT Section 8 (c) of the Selective Training and Service Act of 1940, as amended, and section 9 (c) of the Selective Service Act of 1948, provide that any person who is restored to a position in accordance with

these laws shall be considered (a) as having been on furlough or leave of absence during his period of training and service in the land or naval forces; (b) shall be so restored without loss of seniority; (c) shall be entitled to participate in insurance or other benefits offered by the employer pursuant to established rules and practices relating to employees on furlough or leave of absence in effect with the employer at the time such person was inducted into such forces; and (d) shall not be discharged from such position without cause within 1 year after such restoration. In the Selective Service Act of 1948, Congress declared its sense to be that servicemen should be restored to their jobs so as to enjoy the status they would have had if they had remained steadily on the job instead of entering the armed forces. 

Seniority rights, private employees. With respect to the effect of seniority systems on reemployment rights of veterans employed in private industry or business the Supreme Court of the United States in its decision of May 27, 1946, in the case of Abraham Fishgold v.

Sullivan Drydock & Repair Corporation (66 S. Ct. 1105), held in effect that, while the law preserves the veteran's seniority rights by counting service in the armed forces as time worked and protects the veteran within the framework of the seniority system in effect under private contracts or collective-bargaining agreements, it does not give the veteran "superseniority" or greater seniority than he would have had if his employment had not been interrupted by military service.

Hence, in any lay-off or reduction of force required because of slackening of work, the veteran is in the same position he would have occupied if he had kept his position continuously during the war. 

There are many decisions of the lower courts interpreting the reemployment statutes, and any veteran who is uncertain of his rights or who is in need of advice and assistance in interpreting the law as applied to his reemployment problem may consult either the local employment office of his State employment service or the field office of the Bureau of Veterans' Reemployment Rights in his area, or write the Bureau of Veterans' Reemployment Rights, Department of Labor, Washington, D. C. 

Federal employees.-The rights after reinstatement of veterans restored to employment in the Federal Government are granted by various Federal statutes and Executive orders and under rules and regulations of the United States Civil Service Commission promulgated pursuant to statutory authority. Upon restoration to duty the Federal agency restoring the veteran to employment will take whatever steps are necessary to secure these benefits for the veteran and will inform him fully concerning his rights. 

Veterans who are restored to employment in the Government service are entitled to preference benefits under the Veterans Preference Act of 1944 and to other benefits, including credit for military service toward within-grade salary advancements and toward Federal civilian retirement. These benefits and the procedure to be followed in submitting a preference claim are discussed in part H of this chapter, "Preferences extended to veterans and their dependents in Federal civilian employment." 

SECTION 775. BUREAU OF VETERANS' REEMPLOYMENT RIGHTS 

The Director of the Selective Service System was formerly charged with the responsibility of general coordination of the Nation-wide

program of aiding veterans who have satisfactorily completed service in the armed forces in obtaining reemployment in their former positions, or in positions of like seniority, status, and pay. Pursuant to the mandate in section 8 (g) of the Selective Training and Service Act of 1940, as amended, a Veterans' Personnel Division was established with adequate facilities to aid veterans in obtaining replacement in their former positions. 

Pursuant to Public Law 26, Eightieth Congress, March 31, 1947, veterans' reemployment rights and functions were transferred, effective March 29, 1947, from the Selective Service System to the Secretary of Labor who thereupon established the Bureau of Veterans' Reemployment Rights in the Labor Department to handle these matters. 

Section 9 (h) of the Selective Service Act of 1948 provides that the 

Secretary of Labor, through the Bureau of Veterans' Reemployment

Rights, shall render such aid to returning veterans. In rendering such aid the Secretary of Labor is authorized to use existing Federal and State agencies and to utilize the assistance of volunteers. This Bureau operates at the local level largely through local offices of the various State employment services and approximately 4,000 volunteer veterans' reemployment rights committeemen who serve without pay as the veterans' personal representatives and counselors to advise and assist veterans in getting their old jobs back and in solving their reemployment problems.

If the veteran first goes to a local employment office, that office will usually refer him to the nearest volunteer committeeman who negotiates direct with the veteran's employer. In this connection the committeeman utilizes the services of State and local groups, labor, business, industrial, and other organizations engaged in activities on behalf of returning veterans.

SECTION 776. PROCEDURE IN CONTROVERSIAL CASES 

Private employers generally have been most liberal in interpreting the reemployment statutes and in granting returning veterans all rights and benefits accorded under the law. In case a private employer refuses to reemploy a veteran or otherwise fails to comply with the provisions of the law, and the local committeeman is unable to bring about a satisfactory settlement, the committeeman will refer the veteran's case to the field representative of the Bureau of Veterans' Reemployment Rights for further negotiations. If the field representative cannot reach a settlement satisfactory to the veteran he may, upon the written request of the veteran, refer the matter to the United States district attorney for the district in which the employer has his place of business.

After a veteran's claim for reemployment or other claim against a private employer has been referred to the United States attorney for court action and the district attorney finds that the veteran does not have a valid claim and declines to act as his attorney, the veteran has the right to retain private counsel to assist him if he wishes to pursue the matter further through other channels.

The Bureau of Veterans' Reemployment Rights maintains 10 field offices covering the various States as follows:
