Part H. Preferences Extended to Veterans and Their Dependents in Federal Civilian Employment SECTION 777. VETERANS' PREFERENCE ACT OF 1944 

Veterans' preference is a well-established policy of the Federal Government functioning in its capacity as employer. Since shortly after the Civil War the Congress has provided in various statutes for the granting of employment preference to veterans and their dependents. The first preference statute enacted March 3, 1865, in the form of a resolution (13 Stat. 571) granted preference to persons honorably discharged from the military or naval service by reason of disability resulting from wounds or sickness incurred in line of duty. Laws subsequently enacted provided for the granting of employment preference to all honorably discharged soldiers, sailors, and marines, and their widows, and to the wives of disabled veterans, in appointments to clerical and other positions in the executive branch of the Government in the District of Columbia or elsewhere (5 U. S. C. 

35, 36, 37, 645, 648). 

Under date of June 27, 1944, the Congress of the United States enacted legislation known as the Veterans' Preference Act of 1944 (Public Law 359, 78th Cong.; 5 U. S. C. 851-869), which brought together for the first time the various statutes, Executive orders, and regulations of the Civil Service Commission granting preference in Federal employment. This act included substantially the same groups as were accorded preference under prior laws, except that nondisabled ex-servicemen whose only service was performed during peacetime, and their widows, would not in the future be entitled to employment preference. However, by virtue of the saving clause for the protection of existing rights contained in section 18 of the act of June 27, 1944 (5 U. S. C. 867), the statute may not be construed as taking away the rights of nondisabled peacetime veterans or the rights of any

preference eligible already in Government employment, or whose name was carried on civil-service registers on the date of enactment. Recent amendments to the act have extended preference to certain mothers of disabled or deceased veterans.

The United States Civil Service Commission is charged with the responsibility of promulgating appropriate rules and regulations to carry into full effect the provisions, intent, and purpose of the Veterans' Preference Act and such Executive orders as may be issued pursuant thereto and in furtherance thereof. 

SECTION 778. PERSONS ENTITLED TO VETERAN PREFERENCE 

In certification for appointment, in appointment, in reinstatement, in reemployment, and in retention in civilian positions in all establishments, agencies, and departments of the executive branch of the Government, permanent or temporary, and in either (a) the classified civil service; (b) the unclassified civil service; (c) any temporary or emergency establishment, agency, or department created by acts of Congress or Presidential Executive order; and (d) the civil service of the District of Columbia, preference shall be given to the following 

(a) Service-connected disabled veterans.-Those ex-service men and women who have served on active duty in any branch of the armed forces of the United States and have been separated therefrom under honorable conditions and who have established the present existence of a service-connected disability or who are receiving compensation, disability retirement benefits, or pension by reason of public laws administered by the Veterans' Administration, the Army Department, or the Navy Department.

Disabled veterans are entitled to 10 points in addition to their earned ratings in civil-service examinations. (b) Wives of disabled veterans.-The wives of those honorably discharged ex-servicemen who are unable to qualify for any civil service appointment because of service-connected disability.

Persons in this group are entitled to 10 points in addition to their earned ratings in civil service examinations. (c) Unmarried widows of war veterans.—As long as they do not remarry, widows of deceased ex-servicemen who served on active duty in any branch of the armed forces of the United States during any war, or in any campaign or expedition (for which a campaign badge has been authorized), and who were separated therefrom under honorable conditions or who died in the performance of such duty. 

Persons in this group are entitled to 10 points in addition to their earned ratings in civil-service examinations. (d) War-service veterans.-Those ex-service men and women who have served on active duty in any branch of the armed forces of the United States, during any war, or in any campaign or expedition (for which a campaign badge has been authorized), and have been separated therefrom under honorable conditions.

Persons in this group are entitled to 5 points in addition to their earned ratings in civil-service examinations (5 U. S. C. 851). (e) Widowed mothers of service-connected deceased or disabled veterans.-Widowed mothers (who have not remarried), (1) of deceased veterans who lost their lives under honorable conditions while on

active duty in any branch of the armed forces of the United States during any war, or in any campaign or expedition (for which a campaign badge has been authorized), or (2) of service-connected permanently and totally disabled veterans who were separated from such armed forces under honorable conditions.

Persons in this group are entitled to 10 points in addition to their earned ratings in civil-service examinations. (f) Divorced or separated mothers of service-connected deceased or disabled veterans.-A mother of a deceased veteran who lost his or her life while on active duty in any branch of the armed forces of the United States during any war, or in any campaign or expedition (for which a campaign badge has been authorized) or of a service-connected permanently and totally disabled veteran, if (A) said veteran. was separated from such armed forces under honorable conditions, (B) the mother was divorced or separated from the father of the veteran, and (C) the mother has not remarried. 

Persons in this group are entitled to 10 points in addition to their earned ratings in civil-service examinations (sec. 2, Veterans Preference Act of 1944, as amended by Public Law 396, 80th Cong., Jan. 19, 1948, and Public Law 888, 80th Cong., July 2, 1948).

SECTION 779. PREFERENCES IN CIVIL-SERVICE EXAMINATIONS AND APPOINTMENTS 

In addition to 5-point preference granted nondisabled war veterans and 10-point preference granted service-connected disabled veterans, the wives of service-connected disabled veterans who are unable to qualify for appointment, the unremarried widows of deceased war veterans, the unremarried widowed mothers of service-connected deceased war veterans or of service-connected permanently and totally disabled veterans, and the divorced or separated mothers of serviceconnected deceased war veterans, or service-connected permanently and totally disabled veterans (such persons being referred to as "preference eligibles" where dependents are included), are entitled to the following special benefits and preferences in examinations for and appointment to the Federal civil service: (a) Credit for time spent in military service.-In examinations where experience is an element of qualification, time spent in the military or naval service of the United States is credited in a veteran's experience rating where his or her actual employment in a similar vocation to that for which he or she is examined was interrupted by military or naval service. In all examinations to determine the qualifications of a veteran applicant, credit is given for all valuable experience, including experience gained in religious, civic, welfare, service, and organizational activities, regardless of whether any compensation was received therefor (5 U. S. C. 853). 

(b) Waiver of physical requirements. The Civil Service Commission or other examining agency, after giving due consideration to the recommendation of any accredited physician, is authorized to waive the physical requirements in the case of any veteran, provided such veteran is, in the opinion of the Civil Service Commission, or other examining agency, physically able to discharge efficiently the duties of the position for which the examination is given. 81200°-48- -9

In determining qualifications for examination, appointment, promotion, retention, transfer, or reinstatement with respect to a preference eligible, the Civil Service Commission or other examining agency is also authorized to waive requirements as to age, height, and weight, provided any such requirement is not essential to the performance of the duties of the position (5 U. S. C. 854). (c) Waiver of educational requirements.-No minimum educational requirement will be prescribed in any civil-service examination except for such scientific, technical, or professional positions the duties of which the Civil Service Commission decides cannot be performed by a person who does not have such education. The Commission shall make a part of its public records its reasons for such decision (5) 

(d) Waiver of members of family restriction and apportionment requirement. Preference eligibles are not subject to the provisions of section 9 of the Civil Service Act (5 U. S. C. 641) which provides that not more than two members of a family living under the same roof may hold jobs simultaneously in the classified civil service; or to the provisions of section 2 of that act (5 U. S. C. 633) concerning apportionment of appointments in the Government service in the District of Columbia among the several States and Territories according to population, but may be required to furnish evidence of residence and domicile (5 U. S. C. 855). 

(e) Positions restricted to preference eligibles.-In examinations for the positions of guards, elevator operators, messengers, and custodians, competition is restricted to persons entitled to preference under the Veterans' Preference Act as long as such persons are available. Until July 25, 1952, the President is authorized to add other positions to the restricted list (5 U. S. C. 852). 

By Executive Order 9589, dated July 16, 1945, examinations for the positions of contact officer, assistant contact officer, senior contact representative, and contact representative in the Veterans' Administration were added to the list of examinations restricted to preference eligibles. 

By Executive Order 9738, dated June 19, 1946, examinations for the position of correctional officer, Bureau of Prisons, Department of Justice, were added to the list of examinations restricted to preference eligibles. 

By Executive Order 9889, dated August 8, 1947, examinations for the position of substitute railway clerk, Post Office Department, were added to the list restricted to preference eligibles.

The position of veterans' employment representative in the United States Employment Service may be filled only by "a veteran of the wars of the United States separated from active service under honorable conditions" (38 U. S. C. 695a).

Superintendents of the national cemeteries shall be selected from meritorious and trustworthy members of the armed forces who have been honorably separated from the service of the United States, and who have been disabled in line of duty for active field service (Public 

Law 452, 80th Cong., Mar. 24, 1948). 

(f) Position of preference eligibles on appointment lists.-Persons eligible for appointment are considered in the order in which their names appear on appointment registers or lists of eligibles. The

names of preference eligibles entitled to 5 points in addition to their earned ratings are entered on the appropriate registers or lists of persons eligible for appointment in the order to which their respective augmented ratings entitle them, but ahead of nonveterans having the same ratings.

Qualified preference eligibles entitled to 10 points in addition to their earned ratings are placed at the top of the appropriate civilservice register or appointment list, ahead of all others on the register, except for professional and scientific positions for which the entrance salary is over $3,000 per annum (5 U. S. C. 856). (g) Minimum ratings on examinations. In an "assembled" or competitive examination those persons entitled to 5-point preference need make only 65 earned points to attain the minimum rating of 70 which is required for placement upon the eligible list. Those persons entitled to 10-point preference need make only 60 earned points. 

In an "unassembled" examination in which a written test is not required, the rating being based on experience, education, and training, 70 points is required of all applicants including veterans to which may be added their preference of 5 or 10 points. (h) Reopening examinations.—A person granted 10-point preference may file application at any time for examination for any position he may specify to which any appointment has been made within the preceding 3 years (5 U. S. C. 859). (i) Selections for appointment.-Appointing officers shall, with sole reference to merit and fitness, make selections for appointment to each vacancy from not more than the highest three names certified by the Civil Service Commission for appointment from the top of the -list of eligibles.

An appointing officer who passes over a veteran eligible granted 5or 10-point preference and tentatively selects a nonveteran shall file with the Civil Service Commission his reasons for so doing, which shall become a part of the record of such veteran eligible, and shall be made available upon request to the veteran or his designated representative. The Commission shall determine the sufficiency of such submitted reasons, and, if found insufficient, shall require such appointing officer to submit more detailed information in support thereof. The findings of the Civil Service Commission as to the sufficiency or insufficiency of such reasons shall be transmitted to and considered by such appointing officer, and a copy thereof shall be sent to the veteran eligible or to his designated representative upon request therefor. If, upon certification, reasons deemed sufficient by the Civil 

Service Commission for passing over his name shall three times have been given by an appointing officer, certification of his name for appointment may thereafter be discontinued, prior notice of which shall be sent to the veteran eligible (5 U. S. C. 857).

No appointment shall be made from an examination register of eligibles, except of 10-point preference eligibles, when there are three or more names of eligibles granted 5or 10-point preference on a reemployment list who are qualified and available for the position to be filled. (j) Acquisition of a competitive status.-(1) Any person may acquire a competitive status by probational appointment through competitive examination, or by statute, Executive order, or civil-service rules.

(2) Subject to such noncompetitive examination time limits, or other requirements as the Civil Service Commission may prescribe, the following classes of persons may acquire a competitive status: (a) Any person entitled to veteran preference who establishes the present existence of a service-connected disability of not less than 10 percent, or any person entitled to wife or widow preference under the Veterans' Preference Act, when such person is serving under a war service appointment, a temporary appointment pending establishment of a register, or a temporary appointment for job employment which has been continuous for more than 1 year. (b) Any disabled veteran who in a manner satisfactory to the Civil Service Commission has completed a course of vocational rehabilitation training in the executive branch of the Government prescribed by the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs in accord ance with part VII, Veterans Regulation No. 1 (a), as amended. 

(c) Any person within one of five other classes not here pertinent. (Executive Order 9830, dated Feb. 24, 1947; sec. 3.1 Civil Service

Rule III.) (k) Veterans awarded the Congressional Medal of Honor. The President by Executive Order No. 9628, dated September 25, 1945, ordered that any person entitled to veterans' preference who has been awarded the Congressional Medal of Honor may be appointed to the classified position of contact representative in the Veterans' Administration without compliance with the requirements of the civil-service rules. (1) Preservation of seniority rights.-Any letter carrier or clerk in the Post Office Department entitled to 10-point preference who, on or after June 22, 1948, transfers from the position of letter carrier to that of clerk or from the position of clerk to that of letter carrier, shall not incur loss of seniority by reason of such transfer, if within 30 days after such transfer he presents evidence satisfactory to the Civil Service Commission that such transfer was necessitated principally by reason of a service-incurred disability. Any such letter carrier or clerk transferred prior to June 22, 1948, shall be restored to seniority upon presentation to the Civil Service Commission of such evidence. 

No regular employee shall be reduced to substitute status to accord the benefits of this act to another employee (Public Law 739, 80th Cong., June 22, 1948).

SECTION 780. PREFERENCE IN APPOINTMENT AS COURT CRIER OR COURT BAILIFF, UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURTS 

Each district judge is authorized to appoint a crier for the court in which he presides who will perform also the duties of bailiff and messenger. The marshal for each district may also appoint such number of additional bailiffs, not exceeding four, as the district judge may determine, to maintain order in the courtroom, wait upon the grand and petit juries, and for other necessary purposes. In case the position of crier or bailiff is to be filled by the appointment of a person who has not previously served as either crier or bailiff, preference in the appointment must be given to a person who has served in the military or naval forces of the United States in time of war and who has been honorably discharged therefrom, if in the opinion of the

appointing officer such person is as well qualified as any other available person to perform the duties of the position (Public Law 468, 78th 

Cong., Dec. 7, 1944; 28 U. S. C. 9). These appointees are not under civil service but are in some cases included under the Federal civilian retirement system.

SECTION 781. CONDITIONS GOVERNING REDUCTIONS, DISCHARGES, SUSPENSIONS, AND DEMOTIONS 

In any reduction in personnel in any civilian service of any Federal agency competing employees shall be released in accordance with Civil Service Commission regulations which shall give due effect to tenure of employment, military preference, length of service, and efficiency ratings: Provided, That the length of time spent in active service in the armed forces of the United States of each such employee shall be credited in computing length of total service: Provided further, That preference employees whose efficiency ratings are “good” or better shall be retained in preference to all other competing employees and that preference employees whose efficiency ratings are below "good" shall be retained in preference to competing nonpreference employees who have equal or lower efficiency ratings: And provided further, That when any or all of the functions of any agency are transferred to, or when any agency is replaced by, some other agency, or agencies, all preference employees in the function or functions transferred or in the agency which is replaced by some other agency shall first be transferred to the replacing agency or agencies for employment in positions for which they are qualified before such agency or agencies shall appoint additional employees from any other source for such positions (5 U. S. C. 861).

No permanent or indefinite preference eligible, who has completed a probationary or trial period, employed in the civil service, or in any establishment, agency, bureau, administration, project, or department, hereinbefore referred to shall be discharged, suspended for more than 30 days, furloughed without pay, reduced in rank or compensation, or debarred for future appointment except for such cause as will promote the efficiency of the service and for reasons given in writing, and the person whose discharge, suspension for more than 30 days, furlough without pay, or reduction in rank or compensation is sought shall have at least 30 days' advance written notice (except where there is reasonable cause to believe the employee to be guilty of a crime for which a sentence of imprisonment can be imposed), stating any and all reasons, specifically and in detail, for any such proposed action; such preference eligible shall be allowed a reasonable time for answering the same personally and in writing, and for furnishing affidavits in support of such answer, and shall have the right to appeal to the Civil Service Commission from an adverse decision of the administrative officer so acting, such appeal to be made in writing within a reasonable length of time after the date of receipt of notice of such adverse decision: Provided, That such preference eligible shall have the right to make a personal appearance, or an appearance through a designated representative, in accordance with such reasonable rules and regulations as may be issued by the Civil Service Commission; after investigation and consideration of the evidence submitted the Civil Service Commission shall submit its findings and recommenda-

tions to the proper administrative officer and shall send copies of same to the appellant or to his designated representative; and it shall be mandatory for such administrative officer to take such corrective action as the Commission finally recommends: Provided further, That the Civil Service Commission may declare any such preference eligible who may have been dismissed or furloughed without pay to be eligible to have his name placed on all appropriate civil-service registers and/or employment lists for which he is qualified and available (5 U. S. C. 863 and 864).

SECTION 782. RESTORATION TO APPOINTMENT REGISTERS AND REAPPOINTMENT IN CASES OF RESIGNATION, FURLOUGH, OR SEPARATION 

Any preference eligible, who has been furloughed, or separated without delinquency or misconduct, upon request, shall have his name placed on all appropriate civil-service registers and/or on all employment lists, for every position for which his qualifications have been established, as maintained by the Civil Service Commission, or as shall be maintained by any agency or project of the Federal Government, or of the District of Columbia, in the order provided for preference eligibles as set forth in section 779 (f) and shall then be eligible for recertification and reappointment in the order and according to the procedure governing selections for appointment set forth in section 

779 (i). No appointment shall be made from an examination register of eligibles, except of 10-point preference eligibles, when there are three or more names of preference eligibles on any appropriate reemployment list for the position to be filled (5 U. S. C. 864). 

Any preference eligible who has resigned shall, upon request to the

Civil Service Commission, have his name again placed on all proper civil-service registers for which he may have been qualified, in the order as provided for preference eligibles, and shall then be eligible for recertification and reappointment in the order, and according to the established procedure (5 U. S. C. 865). 

Any preference eligible who has resigned or who has been dismissed or furloughed may, at the request of any appointing officer, be certified for, and appointed to, any position for which he may be eligible in the civil service, Federal, or District of Columbia, or in any establishment, agency, bureau, administration, project, or department, temporary or 

SECTION 783. CLAIMS FOR PREFERENCE 

Claims for veteran preference may be filed by Federal employees or others at any time. If the claim is made in connection with an action to be taken by an agency-such as transfer, reinstatement, recruitment under delegated authority, or reduction in force-the claim is filed with the agency.

When a preference claim is made in connection with an application submitted to the Civil Service Commission, the preference claim and the application are sent to the Commission's central office, appropriate regional or branch office, board of civil-service examiners, or committee of expert examiners. A separate claim must be filed with each application for examination.

(a) Documents required-5-point preference based on war service.- 

Claims for 5-point preference based on war service may be filed on the Application for Federal Employment, Standard Form 57 or 60; on Civil Service Commission Application Card Form 5000-AB; or on 

CSC Form 14, commonly known as Preference Form 14. No proof should be submitted to the Commission by this group at the time of application. If the claimant is selected for appointment, he must submit the following proof to the personnel officer by whom he is selected: (1) Proof of honorable separation; (2) Proof of active duty, if this is not clearly indicated by the proof of separation. (b) Documents required-5-point preference based on other than war service. The claim must be filed on CSC Form 14, and submitted with proof of the following at time of application: (1) Proof of honorable separation; 88 (2) Proof of active duty, if this is not clearly indicated by the proof of separation; (3) Official notice of the award of a campaign badge (not the badge itself), if the claim is based on campaign or expeditionary service. (c) Documents required-10-point disability preference. The claim must be filed on CSC Form 14, and submitted with proof of the following at time of application: (1) Proof of honorable separation; (2) Proof of active duty, if this is not clearly indicated by the proof of separation; (3) Proof of disability. (d) Documents required—wife preference.-The claim must be filed on CSC Form 14, and submitted with proof of the following at time of application: (1) Proof of husband's honorable separation; (2) Proof of active duty, if this is not clearly indicated by the proof of separation; (3) Proof of husband's existing service-connected disability. (e) Documents required-widow preference.-The claim must be filed on CSC Form 14, and submitted with proof of the following at time of application: (1) Proof of honorable separation of deceased husband, or official notice of his death in line of duty. (A notice such as "Missing in action" is not acceptable.) (2) Proof of active duty, if this is not clearly indicated by proof of separation; (3) Official notice of the award to the veteran of a campaign badge (not the badge itself), if the claim is based on campaign or expeditionary service; (4) Two or more affidavits certifying that the husband has not been seen or heard from for at least 7 years, if his death cannot be established. (f) Documents required-Mother_preference. The claim must be filed on Civil Service Commission Form 14, Supplement A, accompanied by proof of the following matters:

(1) Claims based on death of ex-service son or daughter: (a) Official notice from the branch of the armed forces in which the son or daughter served, that said son or daughter lost his or her life while serving on active duty. (A notice such as "Missing in Action" is not acceptable.) (b) Proof of active duty, if this is not clearly indicated by proof of death. (c) Official notice of the award to the ex-service son or daughter of a campaign badge (not the badge itself), if the claim is based on campaign or expeditionary service. (2) Claims based on disability of ex-service son or daughter: (a) Proof of son's or daughter's honorable separation. (b) Proof of active duty, if this is not clearly indicated by proof of separation. (c) Proof of son's or daughter's existing permanent, total, service-connected disability.

A veteran and his wife may obtain preference at the same time if the wife is entitled to preference on the basis of her own military service. However, they are not both entitled to preference at the same time on the basis of the husband's service. 

The wife or widow of a veteran may obtain preference whether she married the veteran before, during, or after his military service.

CONDITIONS UNDER WHICH PREFERENCE IS LOST 

(a) Recovery from disability.—A veteran loses disability preference when (1) he recovers from his disability, unless he is receiving pension, compensation, or disability retirement benefits; or (2) he ceases to receive pension, compensation, or disability retirement benefits, unless he has an existing service-connected disability. 

The wife of a veteran loses wife preference when her husband recovers from his service-connected disability to such an extent that he is no longer physically disqualified thereby for positions along the general lines of his usual occupation. (b) Divorce from veteran.-The wife of a veteran loses preference based on his service when she is divorced from him. (c) Remarriage of veteran's widow or mother. The widow or mother of a veteran loses preference based on his service when she remarries.

SECTION 784. EMPLOYMENT DURING TERMINAL LEAVE WITH PAY- MENT CONCURRENTLY OF CIVILIAN AND MILITARY PAY AND ALLOWANCES By virtue of Public Law 226, Seventy-ninth Congress, November 21, 

1945 (5 U. S. C. 61 (a) 1 (a-f)), any person who, subsequent to May 1, 1940, shall have performed active service in the armed forces, may accept civilian employment in the Government of the United States, its Territories or possessions, or the District of Columbia, while on terminal leave pending separation or release from active duty in such forces under honorable conditions and in addition to compensation for such civilian employment, may receive pay and allowances from the armed forces for the unexpired portion of such terminal leave to the

same extent as if he had not become a Federal civilian employee. This act amends the act of August 1, 1941, as amended April 7, 1942 (5 U. S. 

C. 61a), which granted to Federal employees who enter military or naval service the right to payment for their accumulated or accrued annual leave concurrently with the receipt of military pay or to elect to have such leave remain to their credit until return from active military or naval service. 

Prior to the act of November 21, 1945, members of the armed forces on terminal leave who entered Federal civilian employment forfeited pay and allowances due from the armed forces by virtue of the dual compensation and employment statutes providing that not more than one salary may be paid to any person from Government funds when the combined salaries exceed the sum of $3,000 and, with certain exceptions applying to retired military personnel, prohibiting any officer drawing a salary of $2,500 or more per annum from being appointed to or holding any other office to which compensation is attached unless specifically authorized by law (5 U. S. C. 58 and 62). 

The act of November 21, 1945, further provides (1) that any person who performed active service in the armed forces subsequent to May 1, 1940, and who forfeited any portion of his accrued military leave by reason of having entered Federal civilian employment prior to the expiration of such leave, shall upon application to the appropriate branch of the service be paid, in a lump sum, the pay and allowances so forfeited; and (2) that any person who performed active service in the armed forces subsequent to May 1, 1940, and who while on terminal leave from the armed forces performed or shall hereafter perform services for the Government of the United States, or any Territory or possession thereof, or of the District of Columbia, on a voluntary basis prior to actual attainment of the status of a civilian employee, shall be paid a lump sum equal in amount to the compensation he would have received for such services if he had been regularly employed.

SECTION 785. CREDIT FOR MILITARY SERVICE FOR PURPOSES OF WITHIN-GRADE SALARY ADVANCEMENTS 

Under the terms of section 402 of the Federal Employees Pay Act of 1945, Public Law 106, Seventy-ninth Congress, approved June 30, 1945 (5 U. S. C. 667 (b)) any employee who, while serving under permanent, war service, temporary, or any other type of appointment, left his position to enter the armed forces and who has been separated under honorable conditions from active duty in the armed forces, and who, under regulations of the Civil Service Commission or the provisions of any law providing for restoration or reemployment, or under any other administrative procedure with respect to employees not subject to civil-service rules and regulations, is restored, reemployed, or reinstated in any position subject to the Classification Act of 1923, as amended (5 U. S. C. 661-684) shall upon his return. to duty be entitled to within-grade salary advancements and to credit such service in the armed forces toward within-grade salary advancements.

SECTION 786. CREDIT FOR MILITARY SERVICE FOR CIVILIAN RETIREMENT PURPOSES Under recent amendments to the Civil Service Retirement Act of 

May 29, 1930, as amended (5 U. S. C. 691-736c) the Congress of the

United States has further extended the benefits, rights, and privileges afforded officers and employees included under the Federal retirement system who have served in the armed forces of the United States. 

Credit is allowed for all honorable military or naval service rendered prior to separation from a position under the Civil Service Retirement 

Act, with the following exception:

If an employee receives retired pay on account of military or naval service, the period of service upon which retired pay is based shall be excluded unless the retirement pay is based on a service-connected disability incurred in combat with an enemy of the United States or resulting from an explosion of an instrument of war or unless the employee is retired under title III of the Army and Air Force Vitali zation and Retirement Equalization Act of 1948 (sec. 305, Public Law 

810, 80th Cong., June 29, 1948). An officer or employee must have served for a total period of not less than 5 years exclusive of such military or naval service before becoming eligible for annuity. If the employee is recalled to active duty and his subsequent military service does not affect his rate of retired pay, such service is creditable for civil service retirement purposes. 

Retirement credit is granted only for those periods of active military service terminated by honorable discharge or by transfer to inactive reserves under honorable conditions. Any periods of service not terminated honorably may not be credited.

The period of service credit cannot exceed the actual calendar time. 

Credit for the period during which the employee was in the armed forces is allowed without contribution to the retirement fund (Public 

Law 426, 80th Cong., Feb. 28, 1948.)

No civilian employee of the Government included under the Federal retirement system who during the period of any war, or of any national emergency as proclaimed by the President or declared by the Congress, has left or leaves his position to enter the armed forces of the United States shall be considered as separated from such position for the purposes of the Retirement Act by reason of his service with the armed forces.

A former civil-service employee placed on military furlough who returns from the armed forces with a physical disability and whose condition is such as to prevent his reemployment is entitled to civilservice retirement benefits with full credit for time spent in the military service without requiring his return to a civil position prior to retirement.

An employee who resigns his civil position upon entry into the armed forces may make application for refund of his retirement deductions in accordance with the civil-service laws and regulations. If he later wishes to reestablish his rights under the Federal retirement system, any refund so made must be redeposited. 

Employees who leave civilian positions to enter the military service but do not return to such positions after discharge while being carried

in a military furlough status, shall be considered for retirement purposes to be separated as of the date of discharge from the armed forces. Certain credit for periods of honorable service in the Army, Navy, Marine Corps, or Coast Guard of the United States is extended under the act of October 14, 1940, amending the Civil Service and other retirement acts, as to retirement benefits under the Alaska Railroad 

Retirement Act (5 U. S. C. 745f); the Panama Railroad Retirement 

Act (48 U. S. C. 1371f); and the act providing for the retirement of officers and employees of the Foreign Service (22 U. S. C. 1094).

Detailed information can be secured from the United States Civil 

Service Commission, Washington 25, D. C. 

The Railroad Retirement Act of 1937, as amended (45 U. S. C. 

228c-1) gives credit for railroad retirement purposes for the time spent in active honorable service with the armed forces of the United States during any war service period where continued employment with a railroad company was interrupted by reason of such military or naval service, subject to certain qualifications and limitations set forth in the act. Detailed information can be secured from the Bureau of Retirement Claims, Railroad Retirement Board, 844 Rush Street, 

Chicago, Ill.

Part I. Other Preferences Extended Veterans and Their 

Dependents

SECTION 787. PREFERENCES UNDER HOUSING AND RENT ACT OF 1947, AS AMENDED 

Under the terms of the Housing and Rent Act of 1947, as amended, and regulations implementing such act, veterans of World War II and their families are entitled to preferences or priorities in the purchase or rental of housing accommodations completed after June 30, 1947, and prior to April 1, 1949. During the 30-day period, following completion, residential properties may only be offered for sale or rent to veterans and their families. Such offering must be made publicly in good faith, and a placard or sign must be posted in front of each housing structure during the preference period, setting forth the rental or sales price, the name and address of the person authorized to sell or rent the housing accommodations and announcing that such premises are offered for sale or rent, exclusively to veterans and their families during the prescribed period. The following are defined by regulations as veterans eligible for this preference: (a) Any person who served in the active naval or military forces of the United States on or after September 16, 1940, and who has been discharged or released therefrom under conditions other than dishonorable; (b) the unremarried widow of such a veteran who is living with a child or children of the deceased veteran; (c) a person who is serving in the active military or naval forces of the United States requiring housing for his dependent family; (d) the unremarried widow of a person who died in active service on or after September 16, 1940, who is living with a child or children of the deceased; (e) a citizen of the United States who served in the armed forces of an allied nation during World War II, who was discharged under other than dishonorable conditions and who

requires housing for his dependent family; (f) a person to whom the 

War Shipping Administration has issued a certificate of continuous service in the United States merchant marine who requires housing for his dependent family; (g) a citizen of the United States who, as a civilian, was interned or held prisoner of war by an enemy nation at any time during World War II requiring housing for his dependent family. This act is administered by the Office of the Housing Expediter, Washington 25, D. C.

SECTION 788. LANHAM ACT PREFERENCES 

Section 4 of the Lanham Act, as amended, provides for the disposition of federally owned permanent war housing. Under such act, and regulations implementing it issued by the Housing and Home Finance Agency, it is the policy of the Government to sell permanent war housing for private residence purposes. Wherever feasible, dwellings are to be sold to persons who intend to occupy them, or to corporations composed of groups of such persons, with preference granted to veterans. The regulations provide for three classes of preference. 

The first two classes are composed of veterans as follows: 

Class 1. A veteran who occupies a dwelling unit in the dwelling to be sold and intends to continue to occupy such unit. 

Class 2. A veteran who intends to occupy a dwelling unit in the dwelling to be sold.

For this purpose veteran includes (1) a person (or his family) who has served in the armed forces of the United States on or after September 16, 1940, and prior to the termination of World War II, who has been discharged or released therefrom under conditions other than dishonorable; (2) a person (or his family) serving in the active military or naval forces of the United States, and (3) the family of a person who served in the military or naval forces on or after September 16, 1940, and prior to the termination of World War II and who died in service.

Sales procedures are prescribed by the Public Housing Adminis tration of the Housing and Home Finance Agency and will be designed to give, through public notice or otherwise, an adequate opportunity to preferred persons to exercise their preference rights. It will be made a condition of the sale of any such housing that the purchaser thereof shall, until January 1, 1951, give first preference to veterans in the resale, rental, or subrental of dwelling units so purchased except that such resale preference does not apply where five or more dwelling units, previously purchased by a nonpriority purchaser, are being resold for other than occupancy by the purchaser. 

In the disposal of demountable housing constructed under the Lanham Act the same conditions of sale and order of preference apply if such demountable dwelling structure is to be used on the site on which it is located when sold. Where a demountable structure is to be sold for off-site use, individual veterans have a preference to purchase, after Federal agencies, local public bodies, or educational institutions, and the Public Housing Administration is authorized to alter the order of such preference to grant first preference to veterans.

SECTION 789. VETERANS PUBLIC HOUSING INCOME EXEMPTIONS 

Public Law 129, Eightieth Congress, approved June 30, 1947, provided that no person could be evicted from occupancy of public housing accommodations on the ground that the income of such occupant exceeds the allowable maximum income for residence in such housing project if, in computing the income of such occupant, the maximum is exceeded solely by reason of including in such computation an amount paid to such occupant by the Veterans' Administration on account of service-connected disability.

SECTION 790. PREFERENCE WITH REGARD TO RURAL REHABILITA- TION AND RESETTLEMENT PROJECTS Public Law 563, Seventy-ninth Congress, approved July 30, 1946, authorizes the Secretary of Agriculture until July 30, 1949, to sell to veterans of World War II land suitable for farming contained in certain resettlement projects and rural rehabilitation projects for resettlement purposes.

SECTION 791. VETERANS' PREFERENCE IN THE PURCHASE OF SURPLUS PROPERTY The Surplus Property Act of 1944, as amended (50 U. S. C. App. 

1611-1646) conferred upon veterans of World War II certain preferences and priorities in the acquisition of surplus property from the Federal Government. The term "veteran" as used in that act means any person who served in the active military or naval service of the United States on or after September 16, 1940, and prior to the termination of World War II and who has been discharged or released therefrom under honorable conditions. Persons "released" from military or naval service include persons on terminal leave or final furlough and those whose status has been changed from "active" to "inactive.' 

The War Assets Administration has heretofore administered domestic surplus property disposal functions. However, pursuant to 

Public Law 862, Eightieth Congress, approved June 30, 1948, the War

Assets Administration and the Office of the War Assets Administrator are abolished effective February 28, 1949, and their affairs, functions, and responsibilities thereafter transferred to other agencies. This act also abolished the priorities and preferences previously provided for veterans in the disposal of surplus personal property after August 31, 1948, but continued such priorities and preferences as to the disposal of real estate, and the disposal of surplus real estate is transferred to the Reconstruction Finance Corporation, effective February 

28, 1949. However, Public Law 862 provides that the Surplus Property Act of 1944, as amended, shall not apply to property of the Government which has not been declared surplus under that act as of 

June 30, 1948.

Under section 23 of the Surplus Property Act of 1944, as amended (50 U. S. C., App. 1632), and regulations prescribed by the War Assets 

Administration (Reconstruction Finance Corporation after February 

28, 1949), every veteran of World War II, and the spouse and child-

ren, in that order, of a deceased veteran or of a person who would have been entitled but who died while in the active military or naval service of the United States will be accorded a priority as to all surplus nonindustrial real property not disposed of in accordance with certain priorities established by the act, when such property is classified as suitable for agricultural, residential, or small-business purposes. 

Whenever practicable, property classified as suitable for agricultural use will first be subdivided into economic family-size units, taking into consideration the variations in sizes of economic units in different localities, and if not classified as suitable for agricultural use, will be subdivided into the appropriate units in which the disposing agency deems the property should be disposed of, giving due consideration to the character of the property, the economic use to which it is likely to be put and the objectives of disposition. 

The disposal agency will fix the price of each unit into which the property is subdivided after taking into consideration the then current market value, the character of the property, and if income-producing property, the estimated earning capacity thereof. Before any such property is disposed of, any veteran may apply for the purchase of any or all units offered for sale at the price so fixed. The disposing agency will prescribe the time limit in which application must be filed and give notice thereof so as to enable veterans and the widows and children of deceased veterans and servicemen to exercise their rights to preference. Selection of the purchaser of each unit will be provided for by lot from among applicants for the unit. If any applicant is selected as the purchaser of more than one unit, he must elect which one to take, whereupon the right to purchase the remaining units will go to the remaining applicants in the order in which their names are drawn. The priority of a veteran or the spouse, and children of a deceased veteran or serviceman ceases to exist after it has been effectively exercised with respect to one appropriate unit. 

Sales to veterans will be upon such terms as the disposing agency may prescribe.

In the case of the death of a person entitled to preference rights as a veteran in the purchase of surplus real property, his spouse and children in that order succeed to the rights of the veteran existing at the time of his death. Any preference right to which he would have been entitled except for his death while in the active military or naval service of the United States is extended to his spouse and children, in that order. No preference right may be assigned or exercised by power of attorney or through a power to select except as may be permitted by regulations prescribed by the disposing agency in order to prevent the loss of such right by the holder thereof. 

A certificate by the disposal agency that the foregoing provisions have been complied with in the case of any property and that no qualified applicant has made application to exercise his privilege to purchase within the time limits fixed will terminate all privileges to purchase such property.

SECTION 792. EXEMPTIONS UNDER FEDERAL TAX LAWS 

Payments of benefits to, or on account of, a beneficiary under any of the laws relating to veterans are exempted from the payment of income tax (38 U. S. C. 454a). Retirement pay, except for disability, paid to

officers retired from active service in the Regular Army, Navy, or Marine Corps has been held by the Bureau of Internal Revenue as not exempt from Federal income tax, since such pay has not been made specifically exempt by law and is regarded as compensation for services previously rendered in the Regular Army, Navy, or Marine Corps and for awaiting orders to active service whenever the contingency may arise. An officer who is retired from active service is considered as still being in the military service of the United States. 

Public Law 706, Eightieth Congress, approved June 19, 1948 (amending sec. 1700 (a) (1) of the Internal Revenue Code) provides exemption from the Federal admissions tax in the case of admission free of charge of federally hospitalized veterans and members of the armed forces when the veteran or member is not on leave or furlough. 

Public Law 367, approved August 5, 1947, authorizes the refund of certain Federal income taxes of members of the armed forces who died while in the active service between the dates of December 7, 1941, and December 31, 1947, both inclusive. It authorizes the refund of the Federal income taxes of such members paid for the taxable year in which they died and for prior taxable years ending on or after December 7, 1941, during any part of which they were members of the armed forces. It also authorizes the refund of the Federal income taxes of such members remaining unpaid at the date of their death but paid thereafter for any taxable year ended prior to December 7, 1941. Claims for refund of taxes paid in these cases should be filed with the collector of internal revenue to whom they were paid and must be filed within a limited period, which in certain cases ends on 

December 31, 1948. Copies of the applicable Form 843 and information may be obtained from the office of any collector of internal revenue, but claims and correspondence relative to claims should be directed to the office of the collector to whom the tax was originally paid and should not be sent to the Veterans' Administration or the Bureau of 

Internal Revenue at Washington, D. C. 

SECTION 793. PREFERENCES FOR VETERANS AND THEIR DEPENDENTS UNDER HOMESTEAD LAWS 

Under the provisions of Public, No. 434, Seventy-eighth Congress, September 27, 1944, as amended (43 U. S. C. 279-283), any person who has served in the military or naval forces of the United States for a period of at least 90 days at any time on or after September 16, 1940, and prior to the termination of World War II, who is honorably discharged from the military or naval forces and who makes homestead entry subsequent to such discharge, may have the period of such service, not exceeding 2 years, construed to be equivalent to residence and cultivation upon the land for the same length of time. Credit will be allowed for 2 years' service to any person who has served in the military or naval forces of the United States during the above period (1) if such person is discharged on account of wounds received or disability incurred during the above period in the line of duty, or (2) if such person is regularly discharged and subsequently is furnished hospitalization or is awarded compensation by the Government on account of such wounds or disability. When the homestead entry is made by a husband or wife whose spouse is entitled to any service credit as outlined in this paragraph, such credit will, with the consent

of the spouse entitled thereto, be available to the husband or wife making the entry, in addition to any service credit to which he or she individually may be entitled. No patent will issue to any such person who has not resided upon his homestead and otherwise complied with the provisions of the homestead laws for a period of at least 1 year. No person who has served in the military or naval forces of the United States for a period of at least 90 days at any time on or after September 16, 1940, and prior to the termination of World War II, and who is honorably discharged will be disqualified from making homestead entry or from any other benefits of the act merely by reason of not having reached the age of 21 years.

The surviving spouse or the minor children will be entitled (1) in case of the death of any person as the result of wounds received or disability incurred in line of duty while serving in the military or naval forces of the United States on or after September 16, 1940, and prior to the termination of World War II, to credit for 2 years' residence and cultivation on a homestead entry, or (2) in the case of the death of any person after performing service that would be a basis for credit under the act, to the amount of credit which would have been allowable to such person. The credit described in this paragraph will be available to the surviving spouse, or, in the case of the death or marriage of the surviving spouse, to the minor children by a guardian duly appointed and officially accredited at the Department of the Interior. An entry made by such surviving spouse or guardian will be subject to the provisions contained in the act respecting compliance with the provisions of the homestead laws for a period of at least 1 year. 

Where a person entitled to the benefits of the act makes homestead entry and dies before completing title, leaving a minor orphan child, or minor orphan children, patent will issue to such minor or minors upon proof showing such facts, without any proof as to compliance with the law in the matter of residence, cultivation, or improvements. 

Veterans of World War II and their dependents entitled to the benefits of the act are given the opportunity of filing an application for homestead entry at least 90 days ahead of civilians for any land newly opened to acquisition under the public land laws. This preference extends until September 27, 1954; and is effective in the case of applications under the homestead or desert land laws and under the Small 

Tract Act of June 1, 1938, as amended (43 U. S. C. 682a), which provides for the sale or lease of small tracts not exceeding 5 acres suitable for home site, cabin, camp, health, convalescent, recreational, or business purposes. It is also effective as to any land which the Secretary of the Interior may, without a prior petition therefor, classify as being suitable for disposition under the said Small Tract Act of June 1, 1938, as amended.

Under the terms of section 9 of the Boulder Canyon Project Act of 

December 21, 1928, as amended by Public Law 318, Seventy-ninth 

Congress, March 6, 1946 (43 U. S. Č. 617h), all persons who served in the armed forces of the United States during World War II, the war with Germany, the war with Spain, or in the suppression of the insurrection in the Philippines, and who have been honorably separated or discharged therefrom or placed in the Regular Army or Naval Reserve, have preference for a period of 3 months to enter lands reclaimed by irrigation and reclamation under this act, subject to such

qualifications as to industry, experience, character, and capital, as are deemed necessary to give reasonable assurance of success by the prospective settler. Such exclusive preference rights also apply to veteran settlers on lands watered from the Gila Canal in Arizona and on lands watered from the All-American Canal in California. 

Opportunity for land acquisition by veterans of World War II is a matter of interest to thousands of veterans. The Bureau of Land 

Management, Department of the Interior, is committed to a policy of providing such opportunities to the fullest extent possible on lands under its jurisdiction. This policy is in operation with respect to the public domain in Alaska and forms the basis of a broad program of reclamation and irrigation in the continental United States, designed to furnish veterans with farm lands in the West. 

Veterans interested in securing for themselves a tract of public land in the Territory of Alaska may obtain additional information from the manager of the district land office at Anchorage, Fairbanks, or Nome, in Alaska, or from the Director, Bureau of Land Management, Washington 25, D. C.

Information concerning the possibility of securing free public land in the United States may be secured from the Director, Bureau of 

Land Management, Washington 25, D. C., or from the appropriate district land office. 

District land offices are located at the following points: 

Arizona Phoenix. 

California: Los Angeles, Sacramento. 

Colorado: Denver, Pueblo. 

Idaho Boise. 

Montana : Billings, Great Falls. 

Nevada Carson City.

New Mexico: Las Cruces, Santa Fe. 

Oregon Portland.

Utah: Salt Lake City. 

Washington: Spokane. 

Wyoming: Buffalo, Cheyenne, Evanston.

HOMESTEAD CLAIMS INITIATED BEFORE ENTRANCE INTO MILITARY SERVICE Under the provisions of the Soldiers' and Sailors' Civil Relief Act of 

1940, as amended (50 U. S. C. App. 561-572), the homestead entries and settlement claims of veterans of World War II initiated prior to their entrance into the military service are protected against forfeiture or prejudice by reason of their absence from the land or failure to perform work or make improvements thereon during the period of their service and for 6 months thereafter. If any such veteran is honorably discharged on account of wounds received or disability incurred in line of duty, the term of his enlistment and any period of hospitalization due to same, is deducted from the required length of residence, without reference to actual service, except that no patent shall issue to any such person who has not resided upon, improved, and cultivated his homestead for a period of at least 1 year. However, a veteran honorably discharged because of physical incapacities due to such service, who is unable to return to the land, may make final proof without further residence, improvement, or cultivation, at such time and place as the Secretary of the Interior may authorize, and receive a patent to the land entered.

If any veteran whose application for a homestead entry has been allowed or who has made application for homestead entry which may thereafter be allowed or who has a valid settlement claim dies while in the service or as a result of such service, his widow, if unmarried, 81200°-48—10

or in the case of her death or marriage, his minor children, or his or their legal representatives may proceed to make final proof and shall be entitled to receive patent for such land. The death of such person while in military service or as a result of such service shall be construed to be equivalent to a performance of all requirements as to residence and cultivation upon such homestead or claim. 

Similar protection is afforded veterans under this act as to desertland entries, mining claims, mineral permits and leases, and for securing water for irrigation. Detailed information may be secured from the Secretary of the Interior, Washington 25, D. C.

Citizens of the United States who serve with the forces of any nation allied with the United States in any war in which the United States engages while this act remains in force are entitled to the relief and benefits extended under the provisions relating to public lands. 

It may be noted that section 14 of the Selective Service Act of 1948, provides that the provisions of the Soldiers' and Sailors' Civil Relief Act shall be applicable to inductees and others in the armed forces. 

SECTION 794. PREFERENCES UNDER NATURALIZATION LAWS (A) NATURALIZATION OF HONORABLY DISCHARGED ALIEN VETERANS AND ALIEN MEMBERS OF THE ARMED FORCES SERVING DURING WORLD WAR II Title III of the Naturalization Act of 1940, as amended (8 U. S. C. 

1001–1006), made special provision for expeditious naturalization of honorably discharged alien veterans and alien members of the armed forces of the United States who served during World War II prior to December 28, 1945. It was provided, however, that petition for such naturalization must have been filed not later than December 31, 1946. 

Public Law 567, Eightieth Congress, approved June 1, 1948, added a new section (324 A) to the Naturalization Act of 1940 making special provision for the expeditious naturalization of any person not a citizen who served honorably in the military or naval forces of the United States either during World War I or during the period beginning September 1, 1939, and ending December 31, 1946, or who, if separated from such service, was separated under honorable conditions, provided (1) at the time of enlistment or induction such person shall have been in the United States or an outlying possession (including the Panama 

Canal Zone, but excluding the Philippine Islands), or (2) at any time subsequent to enlistment or induction such person shall have been lawfully admitted to the United States for permanent residence. No person separated from such service on account of alienage, or who was a conscientious objector who performed no military or naval duty whatever or refused to wear the uniform, shall be regarded as having served honorably or having been separated under honorable conditions for the purposes of this section.

A person applying for naturalization under the provisions of this law shall comply with the usual requirements except that— (1) He may be naturalized regardless of age and notwithstanding the provisions of the Naturalization Act of 1940 relating to race or descent (sec. 303) and to aliens who are citizens of an enemy country (sec. 326); (2) No declaration of intention, no certificate of arrival, and no period of residence within the United States or any State shall be required;

(3) The petition for naturalization may be filed in any court having naturalization jurisdiction regardless of the residence of the petitioner; (4) There shall be included in the petition the affidavits of at least two credible witnesses, citizens of the United States, stating that each such witness personally knows the petitioner to be a person of good moral character, attached to the principles of the Constitution of the United States, and well disposed to the good order and happiness of the United States; (5) When serving in the military or naval forces of the United States, the service of the petitioner shall be proved either (A) by affidavits forming part of the petition, of at least two citizens of the United States, members of the military or naval forces of a noncommissioned or warrant officer grade, or higher (who may be the same witness described in clause (4) above), or (B) by a duly authenticated certification from the executive department under which the petitioner is serving. Such affidavits or certifications shall state whether the petitioner has served honorably in an active-duty status either during World War I or during a period beginning September 1, 1939, and ending December 31, 1946; (6) If no longer serving in the military or naval forces of the

United States, the service of the petitioner shall be proved by a duly authenticated certification from the executive department under which the petitioner served, which shall state whether the petitioner served honorably in an active-duty status during either 

World War I or during a period beginning September 1, 1939, and ending December 31, 1946, and was separated from such service under honorable conditions; and (7) The petitioner may be naturalized immediately if prior to the filing of the petition the petitioner and the required witnesses shall have appeared before and been examined by à representative of the Service.

Citizenship granted pursuant to this law may be revoked if at any time subsequent to naturalization the person is separated from the military or naval forces under other than honorable conditions, and such ground for revocation shall be in addition to any other provided by law. The fact that the naturalized person was separated from the service under other than honorable conditions shall be proved by a duly authenticated certification from the executive department under which the person was serving at the time of separation. 

(B) NATURALIZATION OF ALIEN VETERANS AND SERVICEMEN GENERALLY 

A person who has served honorably at any time in the United States Army, Navy, Marine Corps, or Coast Guard for a period or periods aggregating 3 years and who, if separated from such service, was separated under honorable conditions, may be naturalized without having resided, continuously immediately preceding the date of filing such person's petition, in the United States for at least 5 years and in the State in which the petition for naturalization is filed for at least 6 months, if such petition is filed while the petitioner is still in the service or within 6 months after the termination of such service (8 U. S. C. 724).

A person filing such petition shall comply in all respects with the usual requirements of the law except that- (1) No declaration of intention shall be required; (2) No certificate of arrival shall be required; (3) No residence within the jurisdiction of the court shall be required; (4) Such petitioner may be naturalized immediately if the petitioner be then actually in any of the services prescribed, and, if, before filing the petition for naturalization, such petitioner and at least two verifying witnesses to the petition, who shall be citizens of the United States and who shall identify petitioner as the person who rendered the service upon which the petition is based, have appeared before and been examined by a representative of the Service.

Necessary forms and instructions may be secured without cost from any naturalization court, from the Immigration and Naturalization

Service, Department of Justice, Washington, D. C., or from any field office of that Service. 

(C) NATURALIZATION OF VETERANS WHO LOST CITIZENSHIP THROUGH SERVICE WITH ALLIED FORCES 

A person who, while a citizen of the United States and during World War I or World War II, entered the military or naval service of any country allied with the United States, and who lost citizenship of the United States by reason of any oath or obligation taken for the purpose of entering such service, or by reason of entering or serving in such armed forces, and who intends to reside permanently in the United States, may be naturalized by taking the prescribed oath of allegiance before any district court of the United States or any court having naturalization jurisdiction.

Any such person who has lost citizenship of the United States during 

World War II, may be naturalized by taking the prescribed oath of allegiance before any diplomatic or consular officer of the United States abroad. For these purposes, World War II shall be deemed to have commenced on September 1, 1939, and shall continue until such time as the United States shall cease to be in a state of war. 

Certified copies of such an oath of allegiance shall be sent by such diplomatic or consular officer or by such court to the Department of 

State and to the Department of Justice (Public Law 513, 77th Cong., Apr. 2, 1942; 8 U. S. C. 723). 

(D) CITIZENSHIP OF CHILDREN OF HONORABLY DISCHARGED VETERANS AND MEMBERS OF THE ARMED FORCES OF THE UNITED STATES 

Public Law 571, Seventy-ninth Congress, July 31, 1946, amends section 201 of the Nationality Act of 1940 (8 U. S. C. 601) to provide that citizens of the United States who have served or shall serve honorably in the armed forces of the United States during World War II, may have the privilege of transmitting citizenship to their infant children born abroad to their alien wives regardless of the fact that the citizen parent is or was under 21 years of age at the date of birth of the child. Under the law prior to this amendment, citizenship could not descend to a child born abroad, one of whose parents was an alien

and the other a citizen of the United States under 21 years of age, as the citizen parent could not have had the required residence in the United States, namely 10 years, 5 of which must have been subsequent to his sixteenth birthday.

The law now recognizes as a national and a citizen of the United States at birth a child born outside the United States, and its outlying possessions, of parents, one an alien and the other a citizen of the 

United States- (a) who has served or shall serve honorably in the armed forces of the United States after December 7, 1941, and before 

December 31, 1946, and (b) who, prior to the birth of the child, has had 10 years' residence in the United States or one of its outlying possessions, at least 5 years of which were after attaining the age of 12 years.

In order to retain such citizenship the child of the service person must reside in the United States or its outlying possessions for a period or periods totaling 5 years between the ages of 13 and 21 years. If the child has not taken up residence in the United States or its outlying possessions by the time that it becomes impossible for him to complete the 5 years' residence in the United States or its outlying possessions before reaching the age of 21 years, his American citizenship ceases.

SECTION 795. PREFERENCES IN APPOINTMENTS TO WEST POINT AND ANNAPOLIS Sons of persons awarded the Congressional Medal of Honor.— Under the terms of Public Law 228, Seventy-ninth Congress, Novem ber 24, 1945 (10 U. S. C. 1091e and 34 U. S. C. 1038), the number of cadets then authorized by law at the United States Military Academy and the number of midshipmen then authorized by law at the United States Naval Academy were each increased by such number as may be appointed by the President from the United States at large from among the sons of persons who had been or shall thereafter be awarded a Medal of Honor in the name of Congress for acts performed while in any of the armed forces of the United States, provided that all such appointees are otherwise qualified for admission. 

Sons of deceased veterans of World Wars I and II.-Under the terms of Public Law 229, Seventy-ninth Congress, November 24, 1945 

(10 U. S. C. 1091a and 34 U. S. C. 1036a), the number of cadets then authorized by law at the United States Military Academy and the number of midshipmen then authorized by law at the United States Naval Academy were each increased by 40 from the United States at large, to be appointed by the President from among the sons of members of the land or naval forces (including male and female members of the Army, Navy, Marine Corps, and Coast Guard, and of all components thereof) of the United States, who were killed in action or I have died, or may hereafter die, of wounds or injuries received, or disease contracted, or preexisting injury or disease aggravated, in active service during World War I or World War II (as each is defined by laws providing service-connected compensation benefits for vet erans of World War I and World War II and their dependents). The law provides that the determination of the Veterans' Administration

as to service connection of the cause of death shall be binding upon the service departments, respectively; that all such appointees shall be otherwise qualified for admission; and shall be selected in order of merit as established by competitive examination. 

SECTION 796. PREFERENCES IN EMPLOYMENT OF LABOR Under the terms of section 15 (c) of Public Law 377, Seventy-ninth 

Congress, approved May 13, 1946 (49 U. S. C. 1114), an act to provide

Federal aid for the development of public airports, preference in the employment of labor (except in executive, administrative, and supervisory positions) is given to honorably discharged former members of the armed forces of the United States. However, such preference is given only where such labor is available and qualified to perform the work to which the employment relates. 

Under the terms of section 9 of the Boulder Canyon Project Adjustment Act of December 21, 1928, as amended by Public Law 318, Seventy-ninth Congress, March 6, 1946 (43 U. S. Č. 617h), honorably discharged veterans of World Wars I and II are given preference so far as practicable in all construction work authorized under irrigation and reclamation projects authorized under that act. 

SECTION 797. BENEFITS UNDER THE SOCIAL SECURITY ACT Title II of the Social Security Act amendments of 1946 (Public Law 

719, 79th Cong.) provides that survivors of veterans who served in the active military or naval service of the United States at any time on or after September 16, 1940, and prior to the date of the termination of World War II (July 25, 1947, as fixed for this purpose by Public Law 

239, 80th Cong.) and who were discharged or released from such service under conditions other than dishonorable after active service of 90 days or more, or by reason of a disability or injury incurred or aggravated in service in line of duty, may be entitled to benefits under the Federal old-age and survivors' insurance provisions of the Social Security Act in the event of the death of such a veteran during a period of 3 years immediately following separation from the active military or naval service. Such a veteran will be deemed- (1) to have died a fully insured individual; (2) to have an average monthly wage of not less than $160; (3) for purposes of determining the primary insurance benefits under the provisions of section 209 (e) (2) of title II of the Social Security Act, to have been paid not less than $200 of wages in each calendar year in which he had 30 days or more of active service after September 16, 1940.

The provisions of the above paragraph will not apply in the case of the death of any individual occurring while he is in the active military or naval service, or in the case of the death of any individual who is discharged or released from the active military or naval service of the United States subsequent to July 26, 1951.

The provisions of paragraph 1 will be disregarded in computing monthly benefits or a lump-sum death payment payable under title II of the Social Security Act in any case where the Veterans' Administration determines that pension or compensation is payable on the basis of the death of the veteran. If such a determination is made by

the Veterans' Administration, any payments made by the Federal Security Administrator under the provisions of paragraph 1, not exceeding the amount of any accrued pension or compensation payable by the Veterans' Administration, shall be deemed to have been paid by the Veterans' Administration on account of such accrued pension or compensation.

Application for the above benefits should be submitted to the field office of the Social Security Administration for the area in which the applicant resides.

Part J. Special Prisoner of War Compensation SECTION 798. WAR CLAIMS ACT OF 1948 

This act (Public Law 896, 80th Cong. July 3, 1948) authorizes the appointment by the President of a three-member War Claims Commission, which shall, among other things, receive, adjudicate, and provide for payment of certain claims filed by persons who as members of the armed forces of the United States, were held as prisoners of war subsequent to December 7, 1941, by any government of any nation with which the United States has been at war subsequent to that date. The claims are to consist of claims for compensation for violation by the enemy government, or its agents, of its obligation to furnish the prisoner of war the quantity or quality of food to which he was entitled as a prisoner of war under the terms of the Geneva Convention of July 27, 1929. The compensation to be allowed is at the rate of $1 for each day of such failure to furnish proper food. The act provides for payment to the widow, dependent husband, children, or dependent parents where the person entitled has died. The Commission, when it is set up, is required to give public notice of the time when, and the limit of time within which, claims will be filed, such notice to be pub- Ilished in the Federal Register. The act provides that in no event shall claims be filed later than 2 years after July 3, 1948, the date of its enactment. As of the time this manual goes to press the members of the Commission had not been appointed, and appropriations for its functioning had not been made by the Congress.
