Part A. Adjusted Compensation SECTION 801. WORLD WAR ADJUSTED COMPENSATION ACT 

Veterans of World War I were issued adjusted-service certificates under the provisions of the World War Adjusted Compensation Act, 

May 19, 1924, as amended (38 U. S. C. 591-683). The face value of the adjusted-service certificate was equal to the amount in dollars of 20-year endowment insurance that the veteran's adjusted-service credit increased by 25 percent would purchase, at his age on his birthday nearest the date of the certificate if applied as a net single premium, calculated in accordance with accepted actuarial principles and based upon the American Experience Table of Mortality and interest at 4 percent per annum, compounded annually. The amount of the adjusted-service credit was computed by allowing for each day of active service, in excess of 60 days, in the military and naval forces of the 

United States after April 5, 1917, and before July 1, 1919, $1.25 for each day of oversea service and $1 for each day of home service, but the amount of the credit of a veteran who performed no oversea service could not exceed $500 and the amount of the credit of a veteran who performed any oversea service could not exceed $625. The amount of the adjusted-service credit, if the credit was not more than $50, was paid to the veteran as soon as practicable after receipt of his application. Application for an adjusted-service certificate must have been filed on or before January 2, 1940.

In computing the adjusted-service credit no allowance was made to any commissioned officer above the grade of captain in the Army or 144

Marine Corps, lieutenant in the Navy, first lieutenant or first lieutenant of engineers in the Coast Guard, or passed assistant surgeon in the Public Health Service, or having the pay and allowance, if not the rank, of any officer superior in rank to any of such grades, in each case for the period of service in such grades. Holders of permanent commissions, and certain others, were also excluded. 

The adjusted-service certificate was dated and all rights conferred by the World War Adjusted Compensation Act, as amended, took effect as of the first day of the month in which application was filed, but in no case before January 1, 1925. If the veteran died before making application the amount of his adjusted-service credit was paid to his dependents in a specified order of preference, in 10 equal quarterly installments, unless the total amount payable was less than $50, in which event the entire amount was paid on the first installment date. 

The amount of the adjusted-service certificate under the World War Adjusted Compensation Act, as amended, is payable to the veteran 20 years after date of the certificate or to his beneficiary or estate if his death occurs prior to the expiration of the 20-year period. 

Sums payable under the World War Adjusted Compensation Act, as amended, or the Adjusted Compensation Payment Act, 1936, as amended (see sec. 802) adjusted-service certificates, the proceeds of any loan, and bonds received in settlement of an adjusted-service certificate, are not subject to attachment, levy, or seizure under any legal or equitable process, or to National or State taxation (38 U. S. C. 

618, 686c).

SECTION 802. ADJUSTED COMPENSATION PAYMENT ACT, 1936 

Provision for immediate payment of adjusted-service certificates was made by the Adjusted Compensation Payment Act, 1936, January 

27, 1936 (38 U. S. Č. 686-688b). However, if a veteran entitled to receive an adjusted-service certificate had not made application therefor prior to January 27, 1936, he was entitled to receive at his option, either the certificate under the World War Adjusted Compensation 

Act, or payment under the Adjusted Compensation Payment Act, 1936.

Payment under the Adjusted Compensation Payment Act, 1936, was accomplished by the exchange of the adjusted-service certificate for bonds of the United States, registered in the name of the veteran, in denominations of $50 with a value up to the amount of the adjusted service certificate, less any loans which had been made thereon and any interest on such loans up to October 1, 1931. Unpaid interest accrued after October 1, 1931, was canceled. No provision was made, however, for refunding any interest which had been paid. Odd amounts over and above the highest multiple of $50 in the amount due on the certificate were paid by check. The bonds which were not transferrable or assignable, were dated June 15, 1936, and bore interest at the rate of 3 percent per annum from June 15, 1936, to the date of maturity (June 15, 1945) or date of payment of the pincipal of the bond, whichever was the earlier. Bonds were redeemable at the option of the veteran or his estate at any time but no interest was paid on any bond redeemed prior to June 15, 1937.

Part B. Assistance to Veterans SECTION 803. EMPLOYMENT ASSISTANCE TO VETERANS 

Provisions are the same as for World War II veterans. (See secs. 766–769.)

SECTION 804. LOANS Veterans of World War I are not eligible for benefits under title III 

of the Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944, as amended, which title provides for the guaranty of certain loans by the Government. 

Veterans of World War I, however, may be eligible for loans administered by Federal agencies other than the Veterans' Administration. (See, for example, sec. 749.)

Part C. Preferences Extended to Veterans and Their Dependents SECTION 805. PREFERENCE IN FEDERAL CIVILIAN EMPLOYMENT 

Same as preference afforded veterans of World War II as discussed in sections 777–783, 786. 

SECTION 806. PREFERENCE FOR VETERANS AND DEPENDENTS UNDER HOMESTEAD LAWS 

Every person who served in the armed forces of the United States during World War I for 90 days and was honorably discharged is entitled to certain preferences in connection with any homestead entry made under the homestead laws of the United States. Included in the group to which preferences are extended are citizens of the United States, honorably discharged, who served with Allied armies during World War I, upon resumption of their United States citizenship, provided their service was similar to service with the Army of the 

United States for which recognition is granted (43 U. S. C. 272a). 

An eligible veteran is allowed 6 months after locating a homestead and filing the necessary declaratory statement within which to make entry and commence settlement and improvement (43 U. S. C. 271).

The time spent in military or naval service is considered the equivalent of residence and cultivation for the same length of time upon the tract entered or settled upon and is deducted from the residence required under the homestead laws. If discharged on account of wounds received or disability incurred in line of duty, or if regularly discharged and subsequently awarded compensation by the Government for wounds received or disabilities incurred in line of duty, credit for the whole term of enlistment may be allowed, notwithstanding that the period of service may have been less than 90 days. 

But no patent may issue to any homestead settler who has not resided upon, improved, and cultivated his homestead for a period of at least 

1 year after commencement of improvements (43 U. S. C. 272).

Veterans of World War I who made entry upon or application for public lands of the United States under the homestead laws before entering upon a course of vocational rehabilitation or treatment by the Government for wounds received or disabilities incurred in line of duty were entitled to leave of absence from the land for the purpose

F of undergoing such training or treatment, and absence for training or treatment was counted as constructive residence (43 U. S. C. 233).

Death of the settler while in military service was considered as equivalent to a performance of all requirements as to residence and cultivation upon the homestead or claim and his unremarried widow, or in the event of her death, or remarriage, his minor child, upon making final proof, was entitled to receive a patent for the land (43 U. S. C. 

272, 272a).

Under the terms of section 9 of the Boulder Canyon Project Adjustment Act, December 21, 1928, as amended by Public Law 318, Seventy-ninth Congress, March 6, 1946 (43 U. S. C. 617h), all persons who served in the armed forces of the United States during World War II, the War with Germany (World War I), the war with Spain, or in the suppression of the insurrection in the Philippines, and who have been honorably separated or discharged therefrom or placed in the Regular Army or Naval Reserve, have the exclusive preference right for a period of 3 months to enter lands found by the Secretary of the Interior to be practicable for irrigation and reclamation by the irrigation works authorized by this act, subject to such qualifications as to industry, experience, character and capital as are deemed necessary to give reasonable assurance of success by the prospective settler. 

These exclusive preference rights also apply to veteran settlers on lands watered from the Gila canal in Arizona and on lands watered from the All-American canal in California.

Information concerning public land in the United States may be secured from the Director, Bureau of Land Management, Department of the Interior, Washington 25, D. C., or from the appropriate district land office. A list showing addresses of the district land offices is contained in section 793.

SECTION 807. APPOINTMENTS TO WEST POINT AND ANNAPOLIS 

The preferences extended to sons of World War II veterans are also extended to the sons of World War I veterans. (See sec. 795.)

SECTION 808. NATURALIZATION 

For provisions relative to naturalization of veterans who lost citizenship through service with allied forces see section 794 (c). 

A person who was a member of the military or naval forces of the United States during World War I, not separated from military service under other than honorable conditions, who had resided in the United States continuously for at least 2 years pursuant to a legal admission for permanent residence (in lieu of the usual 5 years residence within the United States and 6 months within the State of residence at the time of filing the petition for naturalization), and who met certain other eligibility requirements, was entitled, prior to December 8, 1943, to naturalization on compliance with all requirements of the naturalization laws, except that no declaration of intention was required; no certificate of arrival was required unless admission to the 

United States was subsequent to March 3, 1924; and no residence within the jurisdiction of the court was required (8 U. S. C. 723a). 

For other special provisions currently applicable to veterans of 

World War I as well as veterans of World War II, see section 794 (A).

SECTION 809. PREFERENCE IN THE EMPLOYMENT OF LABOR 

The preferences in the employment of labor discussed in section 

796 are applicable also to World War I veterans.

SECTION 810. EDUCATIONAL PREFERENCE IN FOREIGN INSTITUTIONS 

The Department of State is authorized to utilize foreign currencies and credits acquired through the sale of surplus property located outside the continental limits of the United States, Hawaii, Alaska (including the Aleutian Islands), Puerto Rico, and the Virgin Islands, for programs of student exchange, which will include financing studies, research, instruction, and other educational activities of or for American citizens in schools and institutions of higher learning located in the foreign country (Public Law 584, 79th Cong., Aug. 1, 1946).

Four countries are presently approved for participation in the program. These are China, Burma, Greece, and the Philippines. Applications of other countries for participation in the program are now being processed and approval of additional countries will be announced by the Department of State from time to time. 

Inquiries relative to the program should be addressed as follows: 

(a) Institute of International Education, 2 West Forty-fifth 

Street, New York 19, N. Y., if the applicant is interested in pursuing a general course of studies; 

(b) United States Office of Education, Federal Security Agency, Washington 25, D. C., if the applicant desires to go abroad as a teacher in an elementary or secondary school, or junior college operated by a foreign agency; (c) Conference Board of Associated Research Councils, 2101

Constitution Avenue, Washington, D. C., if the applicant is interested in becoming an exchange professor or pursuing special studies or research, or desires to teach in American primary or secondary schools in participating countries. 

Applications are screened by the foregoing agencies and then forwarded to the Board of Foreign Scholarships in the State Department for final approval. Preferences in approval are granted to qualified applicants who are veterans of World War I or II.

Part A. Application or Claim for Benefits SECTION 901. FILING OF CLAIMS PREREQUISITE TO OBTAINING BENEFITS 

A claim for benefits under laws pertaining to veterans and their dependents must be filed with the Veterans Administration unless otherwise provided. Forms to be used for filing application for benefits are furnished by the Veterans' Administration and should be used when possible. However, any communication from or action by a claimant or his duly authorized representative, which clearly indicates an intent to apply for the specific benefit, may be considered an informal claim. When an informal claim for compensation or pension is received a formal application is forwarded for execution by 149

the claimant and such application will be considered as evidence necessary to complete the initial application. Unless this formal application is received within 1 year from the date it was transmitted to the claimant no award will be made by virtue of the informal claim. If the formal application is received within 1 year from date it was transmitted to the claimant it will be considered filed as of the date of receipt by the Veterans' Administration of the informal claim. 

Application for benefits, generally, need not be sworn to but will be acceptable on the claimant's own certification, shown to have been made with knowledge of the penalties provided by law for false or fraudulent claim, statements, etc.

SECTION 902. EFFECTIVE DATE OF AWARD 

The effective date of an original award of compensation or pension generally is the date of receipt of application therefor, except that death compensation or pension will be made effective from the date of death if claim therefor is filed within 1 year from date of death and compensation for disability will be paid from date of discharge if claim is filed within 1 year from date of discharge (38 U. S. C. 472d, 731 note, 732, ch. 12, Veterans Regulations).

SECTION 903. ASSISTANCE AND ADVICE TO SERVICE MEMBERS DISCHARGED ON CERTIFICATE OF DISABILITY 

"The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs shall have authority to place officials and employees designated by him in such Army and Navy installations as may be deemed advisable for the purpose of adjudicating disability claims of, and giving aid and advice to members of the Army and Navy who are about to be discharged or released from active service" (38 U. S. C. 693c). Section 103, Servicemen's

Readjustment Act of 1944, quoted above, gave statutory effect to a procedure inaugurated by the Veterans' Administration in December 

1943. At that time arrangements were made to place contact representatives of the Veterans' Administration in Army and Navy discharge centers to advise military personnel being discharged because of disability of their potential rights under laws administered by the Veterans' Administration and to afford them an opportunity to make claim therefor.

The Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944 also provides that no person shall be discharged or released from active service on account of disability until and unless he has executed a claim for compensation, pension, or hospitalization, to be filed with the Veterans' Administration or has signed a statement that he has had explained to him the right to file such claim. Refusal or failure to file a claim will be without prejudice to any right the veteran may subsequently assert and will not preclude discharge of any person who refuses to sign such claim or statement nor his immediate transfer to a Veterans' Administration hospital for necessary care (38 U. S. C. 693d). 

It is to the advantage of every person about to be discharged from service for disability and it is the desire of the service department concerned as well as the Veterans' Administration that an application for compensation be filed prior to discharge. Each person who is to be discharged for disability is carefully advised of his right to

file an application for disability compensation (Veterans' Administration Form 8-526) and counseled as to the advisability of doing so before discharge while the necessary clinical and other records are more readily available for transmission to the Veterans' Administration.

When the discharge of a member of the armed forces on certificate of disability is about to be effected, if the service member is in need of institutional care and it is indicated that he is entitled to care and treatment by the Veterans' Administration, he will be transferred to the nearest Veterans' Administration hospital for further care and discharged from service at such hospital. 

SECTION 904. EVIDENCE IN SUPPORT OF CLAIM 

If the application filed is not complete at the time of original submission, the claimant will be notified of the evidence necessary to complete the application and informed that if the evidence is not received within 1 year from date of request therefor, benefits may not be awarded by virtue of that application. Certain facts in the application must be substantiated by proof as set forth in the following sections.

SECTION 905. PROOF OF BIRTH, MARRIAGE, DEATH, ETC. 

(a) Proof of birth, date of birth, or relationship for the purpose of payment of any benefit under any law administered by the Veterans' Administration should be established by one of the following types of evidence and in the following order of preference: (1) A certified copy of the public record of birth or a certified copy of the church record of baptism, the certification to be made by the custodian of such records. (2) Official report from service department as to birth which occurred while the veteran was in service. (3) Affidavit of the physician or midwife in attendance at birth. 

4) Copy of Bible or other family record certified to by a notary public or other officer with authority to administer oaths for general purposes, who should state in what year the Bible or other book in which the record appears was printed, whether the record bears any erasures or other marks of alteration, and whether from the appearance of the writing he believes the entries to have been made recently or at the time reputed. (5) Affidavits of two or more persons, preferably disinterested, who should state their ages, and the name, date, and place of birth of the person whose age or relationship is being established and that to their own knowledge such person is the child of such parents (naming the parents) and stating the source of their knowledge. (6) Other evidence which is adequate to establish the facts in issue, including census records, original baptismal records, hospital records, insurance policies, school, employment, immigration, or naturalization records.

Photostats of original documents or of certified copies of records may be accepted if the original would be acceptable.

(b) Proof of marriage. Marriage should be established by one of the following types of evidence in the following order of preference: (1) Copy of the public record of marriage, duly certified or attested, or by an abstract of the public record, containing sufficient data to identify the parties, the date and place of the marriage, and the number of prior marriages by either party if shown on the official record, issued by the officer having custody of the record or one duly authorized to act for him bearing the seal of such office, or otherwise properly identified, or a certified copy of the church record of marriage. (2) Official report from service department as to marriage which occurred while the veteran was in service. (3) Affidavit of the clergyman or magistrate who officiated. 

4) The original certificate of marriage accompanied by proof of its genuineness and the authority of the person to perform the marriage. (5) Affidavits of two or more eye witnesses to the ceremony. 

6) In jurisdictions where marriage other than by ceremony is recognized, the affidavits of one or both of the parties to the marriage, if living, supplemented by the affidavits of two or more witnesses, who know that the parties lived together as husband and wife and were so recognized, stating how long in their knowledge such relation continued, and such other evidence as may be required by the laws of the particular State to establish a valid informal or common-law marriage. (7) The termination of all prior marriages of each party is to be shown by duly certified copies of final decrees of divorce or annulment, or by proof of death as hereinafter provided. 

The failure to furnish evidence of a higher class does not preclude the acceptance of a lower class of evidence if the latter is sufficient to prove the point involved. Photostats of original documents or of certified copies of records may be accepted if the original would be acceptable. (c) Proof of death. Where a claim is filed on account of the death of a person, proof of death will be established as follows: (1) By a copy of the public record of the State or community where death occurred, certified to by the custodian of such records, or by a duly certified copy of a coroner's report of death or a verdict of a coroner's jury, of the State or community where death occurred provided such report or verdict properly identifies the deceased. (2) Where death occurs in a hospital or institution under the control of the United States Government, by a death certificate signed by the medical officer in charge, or by furnishing the evidence required under (1) above. (3) Where death occurs while deceased was on the retired list, in an inactive duty status, or in the active service in the Regular Establishment of the United States Army, United States Navy, United

States Marine Corps, or United States Coast Guard, by an official report of death from the Department of the Army or Navy, or Treasury Department, or by furnishing the evidence required under (1) above. (4) Where death occurs in a United States Army hospital or in a United States naval hospital, while the deceased was on the retired list, in an inactive duty status, or in the active service under the United States Army, United States Navy, United States Marine Corps, or

United States Coast Guard, by a death certificate signed by the medical officer in charge thereof or by furnishing the evidence required under subparagraph (1) above. (5) Where death occurs in a foreign country, by a United States consular report of death, bearing the signature and official seal of the United States consul or by a certified copy of the public record of death authenticated by the United States consul, or other agency of the State Department. 

(6) If the evidence called for in items 1, 2, 3, 4, or 5 above cannot be obtained, the reason must be shown. If the reason is satisfactory the fact of death may be established by the affidavit of persons who have personal knowledge thereof and who viewed the body of the deceased and knew it to be the body of the person whose death is being established, setting forth all the facts and circumstances concerning the death, including the place, date, time, and cause thereof. (7) In cases where proof of death, as defined in items 1 through 6 above, cannot be furnished, officials specifically authorized to do so by the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs, may make a finding of fact of death where death is otherwise shown by competent evidence. The best evidence, which from the nature of the case must be supposed to exist, must be furnished in these cases. 

Death presumption.-If satisfactory evidence is produced establishing the fact of the continued and unexplained absence of any individual from his home and family for a period of 7 years, and that after diligent search no evidence of his existence after date of disappearance has been found or otherwise received, the death of such individual as of the date of the expiration of such period may be considered as sufficiently proved (38 Ú. S. C. 32a).

SECTION 906. AFFIDAVITS 

An affidavit offered in support of a claim should clearly and concisely set forth the facts to be proved. The affidavit should state the facts as simply as possible in chronological order, and with due regard to any necessary details. Affidavits submitted by physicians should state whether the evidence is furnished from office records or from memory and such an affidavit should contain a complete and detailed statement of symptoms observed and the diagnosis made at that time.

SECTION 907. FOREIGN AFFIDAVITS 

Except as hereafter noted, where an affidavit or document is executed by or before an official in a foreign country, the signature of that official must be authenticated either by a United States consular officer in that jurisdiction or by the Department of State. Affidavits or other documents emanating from foreign countries or jurisdictions where the United States has no consular representative may be authenticated in one of the following manners:

By a consular agent of a friendly government.-The signature and seal of the official of that country may be authenticated by a diplomatic or consular officer of a friendly government stationed in that country, whereupon the signature and seal of the official of the friendly government may be authenticated by the Department of State. 81200°-48- -11

By the nearest American consul.—The document may be forwarded to the nearest American consul who will attach a certificate showing the result of his investigation concerning its authenticity, and such certificate, if favorable, will be accepted by the Veterans' Administration. 

Authentication will not be required: 

1. On documents submitted through and approved by the Deputy 

Minister of Veterans Affairs, Department of Veterans Affairs, Ottawa, 

Canada; or 

2. When it is indicated that the attesting officer is authorized to administer oaths for general purposes and the paper bears his signature and seal; or 

3. When the document is executed before a Veterans' Administration employee authorized to administer oaths, located in a foreign country; or 

4. When a copy of a public or church record from any foreign country purports to establish birth, marriage, divorce, or death, if it bears the signature and seal of the custodian of such record and there is no other evidence in the file which would serve to create doubt as to the correctness of the information shown on the record; or 

5. When a copy of a public or church record from one of the countries comprising the United Kingdom purports to establish birth, marriage, or death, if it bears the signature or seal or stamp of the custodian of such record and there is no other evidence in the file which would serve to create doubt as to the correctness of the information shown on the record.

The authenticity of notarizations of affidavits prepared in the Republic of the Philippines may be certified by a Veterans' Administration representative of the regional office or a Veterans' Administration office located in the Philippines who is authorized to administer oaths. 

SECTION 908. FORFEITURE OF BENEFITS 

Any person who knowingly makes or causes to be made or conspires, combines, aids or assists in, agrees to, arranges for, or in any way procures the making or presentation of a false or fraudulent affidavit, declaration, certificate, statement, voucher, or paper concerning any claim for pension, compensation, hospital, medical, domiciliary, or burial benefits, or for education, loan guaranty or readjustment allowance under the Servicemen's Readjustment Act of 1944, as amended, forfeits all rights, claims, and benefits under the provisions awarding such benefits and, in addition to any and all other penalties imposed by law, will be guilty of a misdemeanor and upon conviction will be punished by a fine of not more than $1,000 or imprisonment for not more than 1 year, or both (38 U. S. C. 555, 696k, 697, 715).

Forfeiture of benefits by a veteran under the above-mentioned provisions does not prohibit reimbursement on account of expenses incurred in the burial of such veteran where such reimbursement is otherwise authorized by law or payment of death benefits if the dependent is otherwise eligible (38 U. S. C. 555a, 715a).

When disability compensation based upon service-connected disability has been forfeited by a veteran under the statutory provisions discussed above the compensation payable except for the forfeiture from and after the date of suspension of payments to the veteran will

be paid to his wife, child or children, and/or dependent parents. Such payments will not exceed the amount payable in case the veteran had died from the service-connected disability. No compensation will be paid to any dependent who has participated in the fraud for which the forfeiture was imposed (38 U.S. C. 555a, 715a).

Any person shown by evidence satisfactory to the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs to be guilty of mutiny, treason, sabotage, or rendering assistance to an enemy of the United States or of its allies, forfeits all accrued or future benefits under laws administered by the Veterans' Administration pertaining to gratuities for veterans and their dependents. The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs, in his discretion, may apportion and pay any part of such benefits to the dependents of the guilty person not exceeding the amount to which each dependent would be entitled if such person were dead (38 U. S. C. 728).

The discharge or dismissal of a person from the military or naval forces, by reason of the sentence of a general court martial, or the discharge of any such person on the ground that he was a conscientious objector who refused to perform military duty or refused to wear the uniform or otherwise to comply with lawful orders of competent military authority, or as a deserter, bars all rights of such person based upon the period of service from which he is so discharged or dismissed, under any laws administered by the Veterans' Administration. The same bar operates against an officer whose resignation is accepted for the good of the service. The bar will not operate against a person if it is established to the satisfaction of the Administrator that at the time of the commission of the offense he was insane. This statute does not apply to any war risk, Government (converted), or national service life insurance policy (38 U. S. C. 693g). 

Part B. Procedure for Adjudication of Claims SECTION 909. BASIS FOR ADJUDICATION OF CLAIMS 

The laws, legal precedents, regulations and procedure, and instructions constitute the basis for adjudicating claims. 

Following the enactment of legislation, the Veterans' Administration promulgates instructions required to place the law in effect. 

In some instances such instructions are supplemented later, and finally many of them become part of the present regulations and procedure of the Veterans' Administration. In many instances separate instructions are not indicated and the law is placed in effect by immediate amendments to the printed regulations and procedure. In addition to the foregoing, questions arising under the laws give rise to opinions of the Solicitor, many of which are precedent forming, and when approved by the Administrator become Administrator's Decisions which are printed for general distribution. In connection with insurance, there are court decisions which govern application of the laws to individual groups of cases. In addition, there are many instances where authority exists to handle matters in more than one way; and in such instances it is necessary for the Administrator to adopt one way, which is then applied uniformly, and which becomes the policy. The general practice is to adopt that way which is most favorable to the veteran and his dependents.

SECTION 910. DISALLOWED CLAIMS 

When a claim is disallowed, the claimant or his duly authorized representative is informed of the action taken and of his right to appeal from the decision rendered.

SECTION 911. APPEALS 

All questions on claims involving benefits under laws administered by the Veterans' Administration are subject to one review on appeal to the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs, decisions in such cases to be made by the Board of Veterans' Appeals. The law provides that the 

Board of Veterans' Appeals shall be composed of a Chairman, a Vice 

Chairman, such number of associate members as may be found necessary, not to exceed 50, and such other professional, administrative, clerical, and stenographic personnel as are necessary in conducting hearings and considering and disposing of appeals. Members of the 

Board, including the Chairman and the Vice Chairman, are appointed by the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs with the approval of the 

President (Public Law 425, 79th Cong., June 22, 1946, 38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations).

Applications for review on appeal (except in those cases involving simultaneously contested claims) must be filed within 1 year from the date of mailing of the notice of the result of the initial review or determination. If no application for review on appeal is filed within 1 year, the action taken on the initial review or determination will become final and the claim will not thereafter be reopened or allowed, except where subsequent to such disallowance new and material evidence in the form of official reports from the proper service department is secured the Administrator may authorize the reopening of the claim and review of the former decision. The application for review on appeal must be filed with the activity which entered the denial of the claim. If application for review on appeal is entered within the specified time limit, a reasonable time thereafter will be allowed for the perfection of the appeal and the presentation of additional evidence before a final decision or determination is made (38 

U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations).

Where there are simultaneously contested claims, one of which is allowed and one rejected, the time allowed for filing an application for review on appeal is 60 days from the date of mailing notice of the original action to the claimant to whom the action is adverse. In such cases the Veterans' Administration will promptly notify the parties in interest of the action taken. Upon the filing of an application for review on appeal in simultaneously contested claims, all parties other than the applicant for review, whose interests may be adversely affected by the decision, will be notified of the substance thereof and allowed 30 days from the date of mailing of such notice within which to file brief or argument in answer thereto, before the record is forwarded on application for review (38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations).

Application for review on appeal may be made in writing by the claimant, his legal guardian, or such accredited representative, or authorized agent as may be selected by him. Not more than one recognized organization or authorized agent will be recognized at

any one time in the prosecution of a claim. The application for review must state the name and service of the veteran on whose service the claim is based, together with the number of the claim and the date of the action from which the appeal is taken, and should clearly identify the benefit sought. Each application should contain specific assignments of the alleged mistake of fact or error of law in the adjudication of the claim, and any application for review on appeal insufficient in this respect may be dismissed (38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations).

When a claim is disallowed by the Board of Veterans' Appeals it may not thereafter be reopened and allowed, except that where subsequent to disallowance new and material evidence in the form of official reports from the proper service department is secured, the Board may authorize the reopening of the claim and review of the former decision. The Board of Veterans' Appeals is bound by the regulations of the Veterans' Administration, instructions of the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs, and the precedent opinions of the Solicitor of the Veterans' Administration (38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regula tions).

SECTION 912. FINALITY OF DECISIONS 

Notwithstanding any other provisions of law, except as to claims for United States Government life insurance and national service. life insurance the decisions of the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs on any question of law or fact concerning a claim for benefits or payments under any act administered by the Veterans' Administration, are final and conclusive, and no other official nor any court of the United States has power or jurisdiction to review any such decision (38 U. S. C. 11a-2,705).

SECTION 913. SCHEDULE FOR RATING DISABILITIES 

On and after April 1, 1946, all initial ratings in claims for disability compensation or pension and awards based thereon under Public, No. 

2, Seventy-third Congress, March 20, 1933, and the Veterans Regulations issued pursuant thereto (38 U. S. C. 701–721, ch. 12, Veterans 

Regulations) will be determined under the revised Schedule for Rating Disabilities, 1945, whether the claim covers a period before or after that date. In any case in which the revised schedule authorizes an increase in the rating previously made by a rating board of original jurisdiction under the Schedule for Rating Disabilities, 1933, such increased rating and award thereon will be effective as of April 1, 1946. Nothing in the revised Schedule for Rating Disabilities, 1945, will be construed as requiring any reduction or discontinuance of compensation in cases rated and awarded under the Schedule of Disability Ratings, 1925, or as requiring denial of entitlement to any statutory award or rating, but on and after April 1, 1946, except as to statutory awards and ratings provided under the World War Veterans' Act, 1924, as amended, as restored with limitations by the act of March 28, 1934, Public, No. 141, Seventy-third Congress, as amended, awards in all cases will be based on the degree of disability determined in accordance with the revised schedule, 1945 (Public 

Law 458, 79th Cong., June 27, 1946, 38 U. S. C. 736, 737).

SECTION 914. INCREASES AND REDUCTIONS IN RATINGS 

It is the policy of the Veterans' Administration to handle cases affected by changes of medical findings or diagnoses, where service connection or entitlement is in effect, so as to produce the greatest degree of stability of disability evaluations consistent with the laws and regulations governing disability compensation and pensions. 

When a rating has been assigned it will not thereafter be changed except to correct previous error or on the basis of a subsequent medical examination showing that the disability has become worse or better, justifying an increase or decrease in the rating.

SECTION 915. PHYSICAL EXAMINATIONS 

Every person applying for or in receipt of compensation or pension for disability must, as frequently and at such times and places as may reasonably be required, submit himself to examination by a duly authorized medical examiner of the Veterans' Administration, including a period of hospitalization if necessary.

At the present time, if filed within 6 months from date of discharge, claims are rated initially on the records of the service departments, without an examination, unless it would appear that error would result from such rating.

Scheduled future examinations for compensation purposes in service-connected cases will not be requested following initial examination by the Veterans' Administration in static disability cases or when the lesions and symptoms have been shown to have persisted at approximately the same level by two examinations at least 5 years apart. 

Where a claim is filed for increased pension or compensation, reexamination will be authorized only when the claim is supported by competent evidence indicating that an increased rating may be warranted by reason of an increase in disability or a reasonable probability thereof.

If, without adequate reason, a veteran fails to report for a physical examination requested for disability compensation or pension purposes, the award of disability compensation or pension in course of payment to him will be suspended as of the date of last payment. Any award of compensation or pension concurrently being paid to dependents will also be suspended. Upon failure of a veteran to report for physical examination requested as a result of a claim for increased disability compensation or pension, the claim for the increase will be considered as abandoned, and no further action thereon will be taken unless and until a new claim for increase is filed. If after suspension of his award the veteran should subsequently report for a physical examination and the evidence clearly establishes that during the period of his failure to report the disability in fact existed to a compensable degree, an award may be approved effective as of the date of the suspension.

SECTION 916. PAYMENT OF BENEFITS WITHHELD FROM RESIDENTS OF ENEMY-OCCUPIED COUNTRIES 

Persons residing in countries occupied by enemy forces during World War II may now be paid pension or other benefits under laws administered by the Veterans' Administration which were withheld

from them or terminated because of legislative restrictions brought about by war conditions. Payments may not be made to any person who was guilty of mutiny, treason, sabotage, or rendering assistance to an enemy of the United States or of its allies. Nor may payments be made to German or Japanese citizens or subjects residing in Germany or Japan. In the event a person entitled to payment dies prior to receipt thereof, payment may be made to certain specified persons if they have not been guilty of the above-described offenses (Public 

Law 622, 79th Cong., Aug. 7, 1946, 38 U. S. C. 729a).

The laws which withheld or terminated payments to foreign resi dents during World War II are Public Law 828, Seventy-sixth Con gress, which provides that after October 9, 1940, no check or warrant drawn against funds of the United States shall be sent from the United States for delivery in a foreign country in any case in which the Secretary of the Treasury determines that the postal, transportation, or banking facilities or local conditions are such that there is not a reasonable assurance that the payee will receive such check or warrant and be able to negotiate the same for full value; and section 5, Public Law 144, Seventy-eighth Congress, which provides that when a noncitizen of the United States entitled to a Veterans' Administration gratuity is in enemy-occupied territory the benefit award will be terminated and such person shall not be entitled to any such benefit except upon the filing of a new claim accompanied by proof that he was not guilty of the offenses above described. 

SECTION 917. GUARDIANSHIP 

Where monetary benefits are payable to a Veterans' Administration beneficiary under legal disability, arrangements are made, ordinarily, for payment of such benefits to a guardian or legal custodian. In the case of an incompetent veteran having no guardian, payment of compensation, pension, or retirement pay may be made to the wife of the veteran for the use of the veteran and his dependents, and if the veteran is hospitalized, payment, under certain conditions, may be made to the chief officer of the institution wherein the veteran is hospitalized. Payment of death benefits to a widow for herself and child or children, if any, may be made directly to such a widow, notwithstanding she may be a minor. The appropriate field station of the Veterans' Administration furnishes complete information and instructions to relatives or friends as to the action to be taken when benefits are payable to a minor or incompetent beneficiary (38 U. S. C. 

450, 739).

SECTION 918. DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION 

Files, records, reports, and other papers and documents pertaining to any claim filed with the Veterans' Administration, whether pending or adjudicated, are confidential and privileged and no disclosure thereof will be made except as follows: (a) To a claimant or his duly authorized representative as to matters concerning himself alone when such disclosure would not be injurious to the physical or mental health of the claimant; (b) When required by process of a United States court to be produced in any suit or proceeding therein pending or when such production is deemed by the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs to be

necessary in any suit or proceeding brought under the World War Veterans' Act of 1924, as amended, or the National Service Life Insurance Act of 1940, as amended; (c) When required by any department or other agency of the United 

States Government; (d) In all proceedings in the nature of an inquest into the mental competency of a claimant; (e) In any judicial proceedings when in the judgment of the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs such disclosure is deemed necessary and proper; (f) The amount of pension or compensation of any beneficiary may be made known to any person who applies for such information; and (g) The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs in his discretion may authorize an inspection of Veterans' Administration records by duly authorized representatives of recognized organizations. 

The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs may release information, statistics, or reports to individuals or organizations when in his judg ment such release would serve a useful purpose (38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations, 817).

SECTION 919. COPIES OF RECORDS 

Any person desiring a copy of any record, paper, etc., in the custody of the Veterans' Administration, which may be furnished under the principles set forth in section 918, herein, should make written application therefor to the Veterans' Administration at the place where the desired records are located, stating specifically the particular record, paper, etc., of which he desires a copy, whether he wishes it certified or uncertified, and the purpose for which the copy is desired to be used. The approximate cost of such copies in cases where fees are charged should accompany the application (38 U. S. C., ch. 12, Veterans Regulations).

The schedule of fees for copies and certifications of Veterans' Administration records is: 

Written copies, per 100 words- 

Photostat copies, per sheet- 

Certifications, each $0.25 គន 25 .25

Part C. Recognition of Attorneys, Agents, and Organizations in Presentation of Claims SECTION 920. STATUTORY PROVISIONS Sections 200 and 201, Public, No. 844, Seventy-fourth Congress, 

June 29, 1936, as amended (38 Ú. S. C. 101-102), which contain the provisions governing the recognition of organizations, attorneys, and agents in the presentation of claims, read as follows:

SEC. 200. The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs is hereby authorized to rec ognize representatives of the American National Red Cross, the American Legion, the Disabled American Veterans of the World War,' the Grand Army of the Republic, the United Spanish War Veterans, Veterans of Foreign Wars, and such other organizations as he shall approve, in the presentation of claims under statutes

administered by the Veterans' Administration. However, no such representative shall be recognized until a certificate has been filed in the Veterans' Administration, under such rules as the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs may prescribe, certifying that no fee or compensation of whatsoever nature shall be charged veterans or the dependents of veterans for service rendered. The rules prescribed by the 

Administrator of Veterans' Affairs shall contain a provision requiring in each claim the filing of a power of attorney in such manner and form as the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs may prescribe. The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs is further authorized in his discretion, under such regulations as he may prescribe, to recognize any person for the purpose of a particular claim under the conditions and limitations of this section. 

Retired officers and enlisted men of the Army, Navy, Marine Corps, and Coast Guard of the United States, while not on active duty, shall not by reason of their status as such be subject to the provisions of sections 109 and 113 of the Criminal

The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs is hereby authorized, under such rules and regulations as he may prescribe, to recognize agents and attorneys in the preparation, presentation, and prosecution of claims under statutes administered by the Veterans' Administration. The rules and regulations prescribed by the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs may require of such agents and attorneys, before being recognized as representatives of claimants, that they shall show that they are of good moral character and in good repute, possessed of the necessary qualifications to enable them to render such claimants valuable service, and otherwise competent to advise and assist such claimants in the presentation of claims, and the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs may, after notice and opportunity for a hearing, suspend or exclude from further practice before the Veterans' Administration any such agent or attorney shown to be, or to have been, engaged in unlawful, unprofessional, or dishonest practice, or guilty of disreputable conduct or who is incompetent, or who has violated or refused to comply with the laws administered by the Veterans' Administration, or with the laws, regulations, or instructions governing practice before the Veterans' Administration, or who shall in any manner deceive, mislead, or threaten any claimant or prospective claimant by word, circular, letter, or advertisement. 

The Administrator of Veterans' Affairs is further authorized to determine and pay fees in allowed claims for monetary benefits under statutes administered by the Veterans' Administration to agents and attorneys recognized as provided in this title and to prescribe rules and regulations governing entitlement to and the amount and mode of payment of such fees: Provided, That payment of such fees shall not exceed $10 in any one claim and in all cases fees shall be deducted from the amount of monetary benefits allowed.

The statutory provisions quoted above were extended by section 616 

of the National Service Life Insurance Act of 1940 (38 U. S. C. 816) 

to the provisions of that act, so far as they are applicable.

SECTION 922. RECOGNITION IN PRESENTATION OF CLAIMS 

An organization desiring recognition must agree and certify that neither the organization nor its representatives will charge a claimant any fee or compensation for its services. The organization must submit a copy of its constitution or charter and bylaws, together with a written statement setting forth the purpose, the work, and membership of the organization and the manner in which ex-servicemen or their dependents would be benefited by such recognition. 

Before an organization may be recognized in an individual claim there must be filed a power of attorney duly executed by the claimant on the form prescribed by the Veterans' Administration specifically conferring upon the organization the authority to represent the claimant in the presentation of his claim and to receive any information in connection therewith. This power of attorney must be presented to the Veterans' Administration office concerned, to be filed in the claimant's folder. The power of attorney must be signed by the claimant, or if the claimant be under guardianship, by the guardian. The form used for this purpose is Veterans' Administration Form 2-P-22, Ap pointment of Service Organization as Claimant's Representative. 

The organizations recognized in the presentation of claims make no charge for their services and do not limit their assistance to their members. The Veterans' Administration in its central office and in its regional offices and installations throughout the country employs skilled persons whose duties are to assist claimants in the preparation and presentation of their claims. While it has long been recognized that a claimant should not be denied the right to employ his own attorney or agent to assist him and while the laws and Veterans Regulations provide for the payment of fees under certain limitations, it is not necessary for any person to incur any expense for services in

the preparation and presentation of claims under laws administered by the Veterans' Administration. 

Any person of good moral character and of good repute who is an attorney at law in good standing and a citizen of the United States, or has declared his intention to become a citizen, may be recognized, if not prohibited by law, and represent claimants before the Veterans' Administration, by presenting his properly executed application on the prescribed form (Veterans' Administration Form 23186). Attorneys who are citizens of the Republic of the Philippines may be recognized under similar criteria in presenting claims to the Manila office. Applicants for recognition as attorneys will be presumed to have such knowledge of the law and regulations as to qualify them to render substantial service. 

Any competent person of good moral character and of good repute, who is a citizen of the United States or has declared his intention to become a citizen and who is not engaged in the practice of law, may be admitted to practice as an agent, if not prohibited by law, and represent claimants before the Veterans' Administration, by presenting a properly executed application on the prescribed form (Veterans' 

Administration Form 2-3187).

Only a duly executed power of attorney confers upon an agent or attorney the right to prepare, present, and prosecute a claim before the Veterans' Administration. A power of attorney, in order to be recognized as valid, must be signed by the claimant or his guardian and must be acknowledged before an officer duly authorized to administer oaths for general purposes, or before an employee of the Veterans' Administration to whom authority to administer oaths has been delegated. For this purpose Veterans' Administration Form 

2-P-22a is used.

Generally no person other than an accredited representative of a recognized organization will be recognized in the preparation, presentation, or prosecution of any claim under statutes administered by the Veterans' Administration, unless he has been recognized by the Veterans' Administration as an attorney or agent. However, any person may be recognized for the purpose of a particular claim, upon filing with the office where the claim folder is located a proper power of attorney and a statement signed by such person and the claimant that no fee or compensation of whatsoever nature shall be charged or paid for the services rendered. 

SECTION 923. AID BY VETERANS' ORGANIZATIONS 

The Secretary of the Army and the Secretary of the Navy, on certification by the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs, are authorized to permit the functioning in military or naval installations on shore from which persons are discharged or released from active military or naval service, of paid full-time accredited representatives of the veterans' organizations specified in section 200 of the act of June 29, 1936 (38 U. S. C. 101; sec. 921 hereof), and other such national organizations recognized by the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs in the presentation of claims under laws administered by the Veterans' 

Administration. Necessary regulations are promulgated by the Sec-

retary of War and the Secretary of the Navy jointly with the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs, and the law requires that in the preparation of these regulations the national officer of each such veterans' organization who is responsible for claims and rehabilitation activities shall be consulted. Provision is also made that the foregoing authority may not operate so as to affect measures of military security then or thereafter in effect nor to prejudice the right of the American Red Cross to recognition under existing statutes (38 U. S. C. 693f).

SECTION 1001. DECORATIONS, MEDALS, RIBBONS, AND SIMILAR AWARDS 

Decorations, medals, ribbons, and similar awards are authorized for individual gallantry, valor, or meritorious service as a member of the armed forces of the United States. Detailed information may be obtained from the service department concerned, at the address listed below, with respect to eligibility for particular awards, the manner in which veterans may secure medals or ribbons to which they are entitled but have not yet received, the conditions under which posthumous awards may be made to next of kin based upon a veteran's service, regulations governing the wearing of lapel buttons, and related matters. Gold star lapel buttons are provided for widows, parents, and next of kin of persons who died in service in World War II. The addresses, all at Washington 25, D. C., are as follows: Office of the

Adjutant General, Department of the Army; Bureau of Naval Per sonnel, Department of the Navy;

Chief of Staff, United States Air 

Force, attention Awards and Decorations Branch. 

SECTION 1002. SIX MONTHS' DEATH GRATUITY 

A death gratuity, equal to 6 months' pay at the rate received by the service person at the time of death, is authorized to be paid by the service department concerned upon official notification of the death of any member of the military or naval forces of the United States from wounds or disease not the result of misconduct when on active duty with such forces. 

The 6 months' death gratuity is payable as follows: (a) If there be a widow (widower), payment will be made to such person only; (b) if there be no widow (widower), payment will be made to the child or children, if entitled to payment; (c) if there be no widow (widower) or child, payment will be made to the dependent relative previously designated by the deceased as the beneficiary to whom the gratuity is to be paid; (d) if there be no widow (widower), child, or previously designated beneficiary, the amount may be paid to any grandchild, parent, brother or sister, or grandparent shown to have been dependent upon the deceased prior to his or her death. As to the group in (d) above, dependency must be shown to the satisfaction of the service department concerned, whose decision as to the fact of dependency is conclusive. In the event of the death of any beneficiary 167

before payment to and collection by such beneficiary of the amount authorized, such amount will be paid to the next living beneficiary in the order of succession above stated.

Information about the 6 months' death gratuity may be obtained from the service department concerned at the following addresses, all 

Washington 25, D. C.: Office of the Chief of Finance, Department of the Army (Air Force cases also); Bureau of Naval Personnel, Department of the Navy; Commandant of the Marine Corps, Headquarters, United States Marine Corps; Military Morale Division, United States 

Coast Guard. 

SECTION 1003. MUSTERING-OUT PAYMENTS 

Under the provisions of the Mustering-Out Payment Act of 1944, as amended (38 U. S. C. 691 et seq.), members of the armed forces who engaged in active service in World War II (with certain exceptions set forth in the act) are eligible to receive mustering-out payments upon discharge or ultimate relief from such service under honorable conditions or, at the option of the eligible person, at the time of discharge or release for the purpose of enlistment, reenlistment, or ap pointment in the Regular Military, Naval, or Air Establishment. 

Persons entering upon active service on or after July 1, 1947, among others, are not eligible for this benefit. Mustering-out payments range from $100 to $300 depending upon the length of service and where it was rendered (foreign or domestic). 

Any member of the armed forces entitled to mustering-out payment who was discharged or relieved from active service under honorable conditions before the effective date of the Mustering-Out Payment Act (February 3, 1944) will, if application therefor is made not later than February 3, 1950, be paid such mustering-out payment by the service department concerned, beginning within 1 month after application has been received and approved by such department. 

If any member of the armed forces after his discharge or relief from active service shall die before receiving any portion of or the full amount of his mustering-out payment, the balance due him shall be payable upon appropriate application therefor, to his surviving spouse or, if none, to his child or children in equal shares; and if he shall have no surviving spouse or child or children, then in equal shares to his surviving parents, if any. Payments to any other person are unauthorized. 

Inquiries concerning mustering-out payments should be directed to the service department concerned at the addresses set forth at the end of section 1002.

SECTION 1004. TERMINAL-LEAVE PAY The Armed Forces Leave Act of 1946, as amended (37 U. S. C. 32 

et seq.), provides, among other things, for the settlement with former enlisted members of the armed services separated under honorable conditions prior to September 1, 1946, for furlough credits which were unused at the time of separation.

Application for settlement must be filed with the service department concerned no later than September 1, 1948, or, in the case of a former

member whose record is corrected after August 9, 1946, to show separation under honorable conditions, no later than September 1, 1948, or within 1 year after the date on which such record is corrected, whichever is later. In the event of death of an eligible veteran, application may be made by certain survivors to whom payment is authorized. The applications, which are available at United States post offices, contain instructions for their submission. 

In general, payment is authorized in cash or interest-bearing bonds as desired by the applicant. The bonds mature 5 years from the date thereof but are redeemable in cash at any time after September 1, 1947, at the option of the holder at full face value plus accrued interest. 

The bonds are nonnegotiable and may not be transferred, except that they may be assigned to the Administrator of Veterans' Affairs in payment of premiums or in payment of the difference in reserve in case of conversion to insurance on another plan or in payment of a policy loan made prior to July 31, 1946, on a United States Government life insurance policy or a national service life insurance policy. 

Should the holder of a bond die, payment is authorized to eligible survivors. 81200°-48--12
